#113. How Ukraine struck the Kerch bridge, a symbol of Putin’s rule

With the SpaceX satellite system faltering, Ukraine’s military launched a surprise, multi-pronged attack to take down vital bridge to Crimea

At 10pm on Monday evening a Ukrainian soldier scrawled a message on a kamikaze drone. It said simply: “The bridge is fucked.” He then added: “We will stand and Moscow will fall.” It was a freezing night, with a bright starry sky and no clouds. Lines of ice had formed on the drone’s bulky wings. Soon, it would be hurtling towards its target – about 500km to the south.

Typically the soldiers who gathered in this muddy field in central Ukraine were attacking oil refineries deep inside Russia. In recent months the work had become almost mundane: they had hit so many of them. The objective that evening, however, was unique. It was a structure personally associated with Vladimir Putin and his grandiose imperial ambitions.

“We knew what the target was. Everyone was enthusiastic and excited,” one person who took part in the special operation, using the pseudonym “Leon”, told the Guardian. His 7th Deep Strike Centre is part of “Magyar’s Birds”, a brigade named after the call sign of its founder, Robert Brovdi, the head of Ukraine’s unmanned systems forces. Leon said: “Nobody gets excited any more about oil refineries. This was different.”

The soldiers got ready to launch, then watched the biplane-like drone, laden with explosives, disappear across the southern Ukrainian steppe.

Over the next four hours, hundreds of attack drones followed the same linear path, flying first over land and then over the Black Sea. In the early hours of the morning they arrived at their destination: the Kerch bridge connecting occupied Crimea with Russia. Putin opened the monumental road and rail crossing in 2018, driving across it in a Kamaz truck. This was four years after his undercover troops – known as little green men – seized the peninsula.

Russian propagandists had described the bridge as impregnable. It was, they said, an edifice as strong and unyielding as their country’s iron leader. For Ukraine, by contrast, it was a hated symbol of Russian arrogance and colonial thinking. Also, officials pointed out, it was illegal. Twice Kyiv had tried to blow it up – with a truck bomb in 2022, and a sea drone the following year.

On this occasion, the Ukrainian military’s careful, multi-stage plan worked perfectly. “It was a well-coordinated operation. Waves of drones started from different locations. The goal was to overwhelm Russia’s well-organised air defence system, rather than the bridge itself,” Leon explained.

As expected, the initial salvo tied up Russia’s local defences. According to insiders, Ukraine then launched a second drone wave. Its function was crucial and different: to act as a relay system for radio signals. Previously, Ukrainian units had used Starshield, the military equivalent of Starlink, for mid-range strikes on occupied territory. Elon Musk’s SpaceX owns the satellite internet network, which is overseen by the Pentagon and the Trump administration.

Ten days ago, however, limits were mysteriously imposed on Ukrainian drones, so they could no longer fly at above 161 km/h (100 mph). SpaceX insists there is no slowdown – a statement Ukraine’s president, Volodymyr Zelenskyy, says is untrue. On Monday night, it appears Kyiv bypassed US restrictions by setting up its own extraordinary and innovative real-time video and radio connection.

Once the relay was in place, a third wave of attacks could begin. This time they featured Flamingos, a potent Ukrainian-made cruise missile capable of carrying 1,000kg of explosives – enough to seriously damage the bridge. An unknown number of missiles – some reports say at least four – were launched from southern Ukraine and sent towards Crimea.

A video from one of the Flamingos shows it drawing inexorably towards the target. The onboard camera captures a ghostly night-time scene. The crossing looms into view against a grisaille background. Suddenly a gigantic steel arch appears. The feed cuts out. Other footage shows the moment of impact: an orange fireball, pricking the surrounding blackness.

Photos taken on Tuesday morning in the aftermath of the attack show a scene of watery devastation. A massive arch had tumbled overnight into the Kerch straight, with a large concrete chunk of the road crossing. Nobody will be driving across the bridge anytime soon. The adjacent railway arch span was also damaged. “We were very happy, swapping messages all night,” Leon said.

One source in Ukraine’s armed forces said the timing of the operation – hailed by Zelenskyy as “unprecedented” – was deliberate. It was conducted ahead of a meeting on Thursday between Xi Jinping, Donald Trump and Putin in the Chinese city of Shenzhen, on the sidelines of an Apec summit. “The bridge is a symbol of Putin’s rule. Destroying it sends a message to the other two great leaders that Putin is not as strong as he pretends,” the person said.

They added: “We understand that what we did shows the world that the king is nude. Putin is weak and vulnerable and we just proved it. The operation was purposefully designed for this specific historic moment. Putin’s plan was to bluff at the summit about how powerful he is. In reality, he can’t even protect the most important and symbolic pillars of his regime.”

In addition to Putin’s political embarrassment before his peers, the disabling of the Kerch bridge has practical consequences. Since annexing Crimea, Russia has militarised the peninsula, effectively turning it into a giant and fortified army base. Since 2022 it has been used as a launch platform for drone and missile attacks on the port of Odesa, and other Ukrainian cities in the region.

Russia’s occupying southern army has relied on the bridge for regular deliveries of ammunition, equipment and fuel. With the crossing partially closed – rail travel reportedly reopened on Tuesday – Russia will have to use an under fire land corridor instead, connecting its Rostov oblast with the Moscow-controlled parts of Ukraine’s Zaporizhzhia and Kherson provinces.

“We have disrupted their logistics to Crimea. There will be a lot of problems for Russia’s army in the south,” Leon predicted. Debris from the bridge strike is obstructing the narrow shipping corridor through the Kerch strait, linking the Sea of Azov and the Black Sea. The Azov sea now resembles a lake. Passenger ferries are unable to move, with the occupied ports of Mariupol and Berdyansk cut off.

The soldiers returned from their mission jubilant and elated. Back at their headquarters, they gathered as usual for a minute’s silence, in memory of those killed in Russia’s bloody war. “We had our daily ritual as usual. Then there were congratulations on a very successful and important operation,” Leon said. Then he added: “We are paying them back.”

Luke Harding’s Betrayal: Trump, Putin and the New Age of Conquest is published by Guardian Faber. To support the Guardian, order your copy at guardianbookshop.com. Delivery charges may apply.

#111. New UK-EU youth mobility scheme may not fully launch until 2030

Exclusive: deal expected to be sealed in principle at EU-UK summit on Friday but trickier elements could be postponed for six months

The new post-Brexit UK-EU youth mobility scheme may not come fully into force until 2030.

It will be a disappointment to any under-30s in the UK and the EU who hoped to travel, work or study in each other’s territories from next year after the long-awaited new deal.

EU ambassadors will discuss the latest draft joint statement at a meeting on Wednesday in Brussels, with talk of youth mobility going right to the wire.

“We aim to apply this agreement no later than 2028 and endeavour to ensure that opportunities under the scheme will be available to our young people no later than January 2030,” a draft text agreed by negotiators from London and Brussels says.

The two sides aim to come to a “supplementary agreement” on tuition fees for EU citizens in UK universities.

The text on this most contentious subject is not yet agreed with two alternatives mooted in the draft text – either a deal that students pay a reduced international fee or, alternatively, the same fee as UK nationals. The aim is that this would be agreed separately within six months.

This is to allow linkage with the Made in Europe legislation that will not reach its final stages until possibly April next year in a vote in the European parliament. The legislation is designed to give priority to EU supply chains in the car and other industries.

By linking the two issues the UK can get assurances that the final detail of the deal on mobility cannot be signed off unless it has been included in the Made in Europe legislation.

EU countries including Germany could accept an unlimited number of young Britons under a youth mobility scheme, according to sources.

Fresh details on how a scheme might work have emerged with EU leaders and the UK expected to settle on a quota of about 100,000 a year being allowed to work, travel or study in each other’s territories.

The UK has insisted on a quota of EU citizens entering the UK under the scheme to guard against mass migration, but some countries have said they will not follow suit on the other side of the Channel and would be open to taking unlimited numbers.

The quota is expected to be worked out on a country-by-country basis with smaller countries such as Malta and Luxembourg seeking a system that will not be based on a per-capita quota per EU member state.

The new agrifood deal would not be agreed until 1 March 2028, the draft statement seen by the Guardian confirms.

Sign up for the Breaking News UK email to get newsletter alerts direct to your inbox

In what looks to be a breakthrough for Burnham there may also be a commitment to enter a dialogue to remove barriers to touring artists, particularly bands and theatres companies that perform in multiple EU member states.

The 72-point joint statement also paves the way for further deals on steel between the UK and the EU.

But there is no agreement yet on whether the UK would commit to dynamically aligning with the EU on trade defence measures such as tariffs on Chinese cars.

The details came as Burnham said there is “no rush” to decide on the UK’s longer-term relationship with the EU, as he came under pressure from his own party to press the brakes on the pace of the rethink.

The prime minister said he would “take stock” of the wider relationship after Friday’s summit in Brussels, at which he also hopes to secure agreements on food and farming and emissions trading.

In an interview with Sky News, Burnham admitted he would now not set out the full options for a closer EU relationship until December – but denied he was “stepping back” from his previous commitments to pursue ties.

#110. As Trump and Xi meet, China's AI dreams face an unexpected hurdle: the Nvidia spy scandal

Jensen Huang is out but his shadow looms over the upcoming meeting between US President Donald Trump and his Chinese counterpart Xi Jinping this week.

For Beijing, the former Nvidia CEO's dramatic fall is a major challenge - and not just because of claims that a Chinese spy may have honey-trapped Huang, one of the artificial intelligence industry's most influential figures.

Rather, the allegations surrounding Huang and his affair with Chinese businesswoman Alice Huang are turning into a new flashpoint in an already fragile US-China relationship.

US intelligence reports claim she had ties to Beijing's intelligence apparatus - in particular the Ministry of State Security, China's main civilian spy agency.

The claim remains unproven, but it has already reignited debate in Washington over exports of advanced chips to China and whether the Trump administration has been too willing to ease restrictions.

For years now, advanced computer chips have been a key battleground in the US-China rivalry because they are essential for AI, supercomputing and military technology.

Chinese officials are worried this scandal could strengthen voices in Washington who argue in favour of further limiting Chinese access to cutting-edge American tech.

Just this week, the Trump administration introduced new export restrictions on Chinese firms that access advanced US chips in third countries such as Malaysia, Thailand or Brazil. They typically do this by renting computing power in data centres abroad to train their AI models. But some of the firms, like the one Alice Huang worked for, are also accused of smuggling chips to China, although they have denied this.

Until now, this controversy appeared to be a sideshow in US-China relations. But with Beijing calling the new rules "economic coercion" on the eve of Trump arriving in Shenzhen, there is a growing risk it could become something much bigger.

Watch: Jensen Huang speaks to the BBC's Faisal Islam in 2025

Democrats, who will control Congress come January, have made their position clear. Last Wednesday top Democrat Ted Lieu told Politico the Trump administration had been "1,000%" too deferential to Nvidia, putting national security at risk, while fellow Democrat Zoe Lofgren suggested Congress would now be more likely to restrict sales of AI chips to China.

The BBC has approached the White House for comment.

Beijing's foreign ministry had described the spy claims as a "smear against China".

"The US should stop fabricating so-called espionage narratives to find pretexts for imposing technological blockades," spokesperson Mao Ning said last week.

The ministry also seemed keen to keep the meeting on track, saying the two countries had kept "close communication regarding interactions between the two heads of state".

For the last year, Trump and Xi have been seeking to stabilise relations after a damaging tariff war that almost brought trade between the two countries to a standstill.

The Trump administration's decision to permit sales of advanced Nvidia H200 chips to China in late 2025 was seen as evidence that both sides were searching for areas of co-operation, even while competition intensified.

It was a rare move given Washington had spent years trying to limit China's access to the most advanced chips.

Many in the US have long viewed these chips as a national security issue, arguing they could strengthen China's military and surveillance capabilities. Jensen Huang, who rejects that claim, advocated lifting bans on the export of high-performing chips to China. Beijing argues the restrictions are an attempt to contain its rise.

History has taught Beijing and Washington that spy scandals rarely remain confined to the intelligence world.

Analysts in China already see echoes of an incident in 2023 when a suspected surveillance balloon drifted through the skies over the US before an American fighter jet shot it down. Beijing insisted it was a civilian meteorological airship that had strayed off course.

The suspected Chinese spy balloon was shot down days after it was spotted over the US

But a planned trip by then US Secretary of State Antony Blinken was cancelled and communication between the two governments deteriorated sharply.

The incident showed how quickly a security dispute could overwhelm diplomatic efforts and push relations into a deep freeze.

"The mere passage of a balloon sent American public opinion and politicians into a state of high alert, as if a "Chinese threat" were looming directly overhead," said the state media outlet, the Global Times this week as it made a direct comparison with the Nvidia scandal.

"No-one on the US side has ever apologised or taken responsibility for the genuine damage this caused to US-China relations," it added.

The state-run outlet also questioned why the Nvidia story had emerged so soon after "the hard-won detente" between Beijing and Washington, following a meeting between the two countries' leaders in September.

"Is someone behind the scenes pushing an agenda and stirring up confrontation?" it asked.

Both Trump and Xi have significant incentives to keep talking.

China's economy remains under pressure and is increasingly reliant on exports. Xi needs trade to keep flowing and this meeting is a key stepping stone to replacing the current trade truce that is due to expire in January.

Meanwhile Trump has repeatedly touted his close relationship with Xi and argued that maintaining dialogue is essential to managing competition between the two powers.

The US president is in his last two years and faces an uphill struggle to pass domestic legislation after the midterms. To counter this, he could be looking for some foreign policy wins.

He may also hope for Xi's help to end the war in the Middle East.

Neither side will want to be discussing intelligence concerns and technology controls instead of trade, investment and economic co-operation.

The summit is also being carefully choreographed by Beijing to show off its technological prowess in Shenzhen - a deeply symbolic choice of city.

It's home to some of the country's biggest tech companies and has become a showcase for Beijing's ambitions in AI and advanced manufacturing.

Also, given Washington's tighter restrictions on advanced chips, Beijing has options for retaliation.

One of its most powerful tools is its dominance of key rare earth minerals. China processes much of the world's supply for materials that are used in everything from electric vehicles and smartphones to military equipment.

Analysts have long warned that technological restrictions can trigger a cycle of retaliation in which each side reaches for its strongest economic leverage.

That would shift the current trend of US-China relations from engagement back towards mutual suspicion.

#109. Britain's pound is beating every other major currency this year

The British pound crashed to a record low last fall as investors rebelled against budget plans by former Prime Minister Liz Truss. Now, it’s enjoying a comeback.

Sterling hit its highest level against the US dollar in 10 months on Tuesday, topping $1.25 for the first time since June 2022. The pound, which has advanced about 3.3% versus the greenback since the start of 2023, is the best-performing currency among developed economies this year.

The UK currency has been boosted by indications the country’s economy is holding up better than expected. Activity is now thought to have expanded 0.1% in the final three months of last year, up from a previous estimate of no growth at all. Gross domestic product growth in January has been estimated at 0.3% after dropping 0.5% in December.

This resilience is bolstering expectations the Bank of England will maintain aggressive interest rate hikes despite concerns about the health of the global banking sector. Rising rates can boost the domestic currency because they help attract foreign investors searching for higher returns.

Inflation in the United Kingdom also jumped to an annual rate of 10.4% in February, underscoring the need for the Bank of England to maintain its tough approach.

The pound plunged close to $1.03 in September 2022 after the Truss government unveiled plans to boost borrowing while slashing taxes, unleashing panic in financial markets that fueled fears of a recession in the United Kingdom.

The International Monetary Fund predicted in January that the UK economy would contract by 0.6% this year, while all other advanced economies would grow, if only slightly.

“There was a lot of pessimism being priced into the pound,” said Francesco Pesole, a currency strategist at ING.

But the sharp pullback in energy prices and China’s reopening have provided some relief about the economic outlook since the start of the year.

“There was a big re-rating of growth expectations around Europe, and that impacted the UK,” Pesole said.

The euro has also been lifted by these dynamics, rising 2.3% against the US dollar in 2023. The pound’s rally has been sharper in large part because its 2022 declines were more severe, according to Pesole.

Both currencies have been aided by the greenback’s sharp drop from highs reached last September as recession fears have percolated in the United States.

A lack of clarity around the Federal Reserve’s next steps has also restrained the dollar in recent weeks. Investor speculation has increased that the Fed could pause or stop rate hikes due to concerns about the economy following the failure of Silicon Valley Bank last month.

Jordan Rochester, a currency strategist at Nomura, said he thinks the pound could rise to $1.30 this year and “potentially higher.” But he still sees risks given the uncertainty surrounding the Bank of England’s plans and how rate rises will feed back through the country’s economy. And Pesole cautioned that currency fluctuations are often overdone when markets are choppy, as they are now.

“In a volatile market environment, moves are exacerbated,” he said.

#106. Deutsche Bank under fire after internal documents made public

A cache of internal records outlines the bank's dealings with Jeffrey Epstein, Donald Trump and Russian oligarchs. The German lender has sought to deflect criticism by claiming its bad behavior is a thing of the past.

Deutsche Bank is in damage control mode again after an international consortium of more than 200 investigative journalists published internal documents from the global lender on Tuesday under the name "Inside Deutsche Bank."

The documents, published in Der Spiegel, Financial Times, Le Monde and The New York Times, among others, cover Deutsche Bank's business dealings with convicted sex offender Jeffrey Epstein, now US President Donald Trump, and a host of Russian oligarchs.

Gleaned from nearly 15 million documents leaked to a journalist from India and another from South Africa, the e-mails and meeting minutes trace the bank's aggressive pursuit of business deals in Russia, and with high-profile Russian oligarchs, right up until Moscow launched its full-scale invasion of Ukraine in 2022 — despite risk warnings from numerous managers.

Deutsche Bank CEO Christian Sewing was among the executives who took part in the bank's Russia strategy working group. The bank also still employs more than 160 people in Russia and generated 2025 pre-tax profits of €61 million ($67 million) there through a local subsidiary.

To view this video please enable JavaScript, and consider upgrading to a web browser that supports HTML5 video

In all, the documents cover the past 20 years of the German lender's business dealings and raise questions about the efficacy of internal controls by showing that Deutsche Bank had repeatedly put financial interests before international sanctions compliance and anti-money laundering rules.

Other red flags raised in Tuesday's reports suggest that Deutsche Bank sought to set up tax-optimization schemes for wealthy clients, that criminal networks may have been able to move illicit cash through the institution, that it helped facilitate arms deals, that it helped companies in environmentally damaging industries and that it held funds for the far-right populist Alternative for Germany (AfD) party.

Beyond more recent negative headlines involving alleged anti-money laundering and compliance failures, Deutsche Bank has been trying to restore its public image ever since its involvement in the US subprime mortgage rate scandal helped trigger the 2008 global financial crisis — a fiasco that ultimately cost the bank more than €20 billion in penalties and settlements.

When Sewing later took over in 2018, he promised to turn the institution around. Although he has made Deutsche Bank profitable once again, he did so at the cost of massive job cuts, and some compliance failings have continued.

Sewing to the German business daily Handelsblatt that some mistakes had been made under his leadership.

"When you look at the events today, you naturally ask yourself whether you could have done some things differently," he told the paper. "I am not without mistakes," he said, but added that the course he set for the bank "has always been right and remains so."

The bank's shares fell by up to 6% in Tuesday trading.

Deutsche Bank issued a statement claiming that the reports were old news and that the institution had long ago turned over a new leaf.

In an e-mailed statement to the press, Deutsche Bank Supervisory Board Chairman Alexander Wynaendts wrote, "During my tenure I haven't seen a single decision from Christian Sewing that I was not comfortable with."

A Tuesday statement from the bank described the revelations as "legacy" matters and claimed they would have no lasting negative financial impact on the institution.

Nevertheless, German Finance Minister and Vice Chancellor Lars Klingbeil on Tuesday called on Deutsche Bank to "provide full transparency regarding how it has addressed past failures and internal control deficiencies and how it will effectively prevent these in the future."

Further, Klingbeil said authorities must investigate and "take necessary action" where appropriate.

#105. Brexit work travel rules need urgent improvement, say UK business leaders

CBI says restrictions on cross-Channel work trips are hindering investment and trade between UK and Europe

Business chiefs have called on the UK government and the European Commission to urgently ease Brexit restrictions on business travel for cross-Channel work trips.

A group representing industry bosses said it wants commitments from Andy Burnham and European political leaders that the restrictions will be one of the priorities in 2027 for furthering ties between the two sides, after a summit in Brussels this week.

The Confederation of British Industry (CBI), which represents some of Britain’s largest companies, welcomed the prime minister’s recent calls for a reset with Europe, designed to reverse some of the damage to Britain’s economy caused by Brexit.

But it said Brexit restrictions, which include making employees obtain work permits for travel, are deterring cross-border investors and hampering trade at the very time both sides are trying to build resilience against China.

Sean McGuire, the CBI’s director of Europe and international, said discussions should have been a priority in the talks this week, after a UK-EU summit in 2025 held by Keir Starmer also referenced improving business mobility.

“Very little has been achieved. In fact, practically nothing has been achieved. The emphasis was put on youth mobility,” said McGuire.

EU diplomats have said in the past that the UK pushed hard for improving business mobility but was met with resistance from the bloc, where member states see British services – a big export particularly in the financial sector – as a threat.

McGuire said that energy, defence and car industry bosses say they are being hampered by the inability to travel freely in Europe to service or run multinational operations.

He said the issue should be of interest to all sides as they build on multiple existing laws to protect European supply chains.

“Now that we’ve got youth mobility potentially in the Friday deal, we should move seriously to address the kind of business mobility, which would apply to both the goods sector and the services sector,” he said.

Get set for the working day – we'll point you to all the business news and analysis you need every morning

British citizens travelling to the EU post-Brexit are restricted to 90 days at a time out of a maximum of 180 days a year, limiting companies’ ability to promote employees and travel.

“If you are a CEO of a multinational you can very quickly clock up 90 days,” McGuire said.

Separately, work permits have to be obtained for each country that a worker travels to. That can affect the servicing of equipment, particularly in engineering, where products that cost millions such as lifts, escalators and factory machines come with service and maintenance contracts.

“There is a big difference between irregular migration, which needs to be tackled, and business mobility, which is bringing investment into the UK and vice versa,” he said.

While an agreement is close on youth mobility, Burnham faces the same roadblock when it comes to opportunities for under-30s, who will be offered a three-year visa scheme to work, travel or study.

“It is a trick because the UK is negotiating with Brussels but work permits are a national competency,” said one EU diplomat.

But McGuire said there is a quick fix for business mobility, involving changes to the annex of the EU-UK trade and cooperation agreement, which allows the two sides to expand visa-free business travel across more industries without reworking the main treaty.

The CBI said it is also “disappointed” that no progress has been made on mutual recognition of qualifications allowing British or EU professionals to practise in each other’s territories as they did before Brexit.

Sources said there was a view in Brussels that mutual recognition of qualifications was more beneficial to the UK than to the EU, causing energy around the talks to drain away.

#102. EU, ASEAN pursue deeper trade ties, but hurdles remain as businesses struggle with informal barriers

2027 could be a landmark year for EU–Southeast Asia trade, with several deals nearing completion. But the harder task will be to ensure that businesses actually use them.

The European Union and the Association of Southeast Asian Nations (ASEAN) could be heading for a breakthrough year in 2027, when the two sides mark 50 years of diplomatic ties.

A long-awaited trade agreement with Indonesia is expected to take effect, while a deal with the Philippines is nearing completion and negotiations with Thailand and Malaysia are making progress. By the end of next year, the EU could have trade deals in place with six Southeast Asian states, up from just two today.

The anniversary could also revive the long-standing ambition of a broader trade agreement between the EU and ASEAN.

On November 17, the European Commission hosted a dialogue in Brussels to discuss trade relations with Southeast Asia, including progress on existing and pending free trade agreements (FTAs).

But attention is turning to what comes next. "Finalizing FTAs is only the beginning, not the end," Chris Humphrey, executive director of the EU-ASEAN Business Council, told DW.

FTAs can reduce tariffs and remove trade restrictions, but that is of little use if businesses don't know how to use them, he added.

"Unfortunately, in many cases, this is where there is a failure. Communication and training are key," Humphrey said.

To view this video please enable JavaScript, and consider upgrading to a web browser that supports HTML5 video

For Brussels, the priority should now be to tackle the less conspicuous obstacles to trade, he stressed.

Cutting customs delays, simplifying regulations, and helping smaller European businesses enter unfamiliar Southeast Asian markets will determine whether an FTA succeeds.

"European companies with little or no experience in Southeast Asia would benefit from greater on-the-ground support, particularly practical, firsthand guidance on informal business practices and the unwritten local rules," Alfred Gerstl, an associate professor at Palacky University Olomouc in the Czech Republic, told DW.

One option is to expand the assistance offered by European chambers of commerce, trade delegations, and EU offices in Southeast Asia. This could range from helping firms find distributors to explaining local licensing requirements and how to qualify for preferential tariffs.

In Vietnam, for instance, sustained pressure from Brussels has helped improve market access for European pharmaceutical firms. In Indonesia, the EU has worked on trade facilitation while pressing Jakarta over import restrictions and halal certification requirements, said Camille Nessel, a researcher at the Universite Libre de Bruxelles.

Regular consultations with businesses on both sides could also help Brussels spot problems before they become trade disputes.

Trade policy has also acquired a geopolitical purpose in Brussels. "If trade policy is expected to reduce strategic dependencies, we are asking it to deliver on objectives that remain insufficiently defined, let alone demonstrating how to achieve them," Nessel told DW.

The EU wants to reduce its dependence on a handful of suppliers for the minerals needed for batteries and other clean technologies, while Southeast Asian producers, particularly Indonesia, want foreign investors to help develop domestic processing industries rather than merely buy raw materials.

The EU and Indonesia have already clashed over Jakarta's restrictions on nickel exports.

European investment in processing facilities, agreements on environmental standards, and greater regulatory certainty would give both sides concrete gains, said Nessel. "The real value of an FTA is not just opening a market but giving the EU the leverage to keep it open," she added.

Pasha Hsieh, Lee Kong Chian Professor of Law and Jean Monnet Chair at Singapore Management University, said Brussels should prioritize ratifying its investment protection agreements with Singapore and Vietnam.

Signed in 2018 and 2019, respectively, the agreements still require ratification by all EU member states before entering into force. "These delays underline the need to follow through on negotiated commitments," Hsieh said.

The issue is particularly relevant as the EU looks to develop investment protocols in other Southeast Asian states where investors have sometimes faced uncertainty over legal protections. Indonesia, for instance, has terminated numerous bilateral investment treaties, including agreements with the Netherlands and Spain.

A larger question is whether the EU should continue pursuing bilateral deals with Southeast Asian countries or revive its original ambition of a region-to-region agreement with ASEAN.

The EU and ASEAN launched negotiations for a region-wide FTA in 2007 but suspended them two years later amid disagreements over wide economic disparities among ASEAN members and political concerns. Technical discussions resumed in 2017, but little has come of them.

ASEAN already has regional agreements with China and several other economies. Beijing upgraded its free trade agreement with ASEAN in October 2025.

To view this video please enable JavaScript, and consider upgrading to a web browser that supports HTML5 video

Region-to-region frameworks extend beyond trade. Humphrey, of the EU-ASEAN Business Council, called for negotiations to begin on a region-to-region Digital Trade Agreement and Investment Protection Agreement.

The EU-Singapore Digital Trade Agreement, which entered into force in February 2026, offers a possible model for wider cooperation, he noted, adding that common approaches to customs procedures, supply-chain standards, and green technologies could create greater alignment with ASEAN members that are not yet ready for full FTAs.

"European businesses have long complained of operating at a disadvantage due to the lack of a region-to-region deal. It is high time that both sides elevated their ambitions and embraced the idea of working towards such an agreement," Humphrey said.

The 2027 anniversary would be a convenient deadline for agreeing on what comes next, but Gerstl warned against mistaking another round of diplomatic ceremony for progress.

"There is a risk that attention will focus almost exclusively on the 2027 summit in Singapore and that the group photo will become the defining image of the event, only for momentum to fade in the years that follow," he added.

#101. Paul Kelly pleads for Albanese to ‘show some backbone’ over AI as creatives campaign for copyright protections

Music legend is joined by Jimmy Barnes and Peter Garrett in backing Not AI’s To Take campaign ahead of Aria awards

Get our breaking news email, free app or daily news podcast

Australia’s creative community has launched a major campaign pleading with the Albanese government not to water down copyright law amid furious lobbying from AI companies, ahead of Wednesday night’s Aria awards.

Music legends Jimmy Barnes, Paul Kelly and Peter Garrett are among those backing the Not AI’s To Take campaign, supported by dozens of creative groups representing the recording, writing, publishing, film, television and media industries.

While the government maintains it will not undermine copyright law, the industry minister, Tim Ayres, said on Tuesday “the status quo is no good” for the creative sector, ahead of a package of AI legislation expected by early 2027.

But creatives have pushed back on such claims, arguing copyright law doesn’t need updating – just better enforcement, and that changes could put the $70bn creative industry and its 600,000 workers at risk.

“[AI platforms] haven’t made one phone call yet. They haven’t called us. They haven’t called anybody,” said Dean Ormston, CEO of music rights organisation Apra Amcos.

“There’s nothing wrong with the copyright framework. The problem is getting somebody to sit at the table and have a discussion with us.”

Not AI’s To Take is backed by the likes of Aria, the Copyright Agency, the Australian Directors’ Guild, Screen Producers Australia, the Australian Society of Authors, the Australian Writers’ Guild, and media organisations including Foxtel, Free TV, Nine, Seven and Guardian Australia.

Australian musicians including Montaigne and KLP have backed the campaign online, while Guardian Australia understands Barnes, Kelly and Garrett – some of Anthony Albanese’s favourite musicians – will also feature.

“Our government has been a huge supporter of Australian music and creativity, and now we’re asking them to continue that support by standing firm on copyright and making sure big tech doesn’t get to rewrite the rules at the expense of creators,” said KLP, real name Kristy Lee Peters.

In quotes shared on the campaign’s website, Barnes said: “I think that these companies should go and f..k themselves.”

Kelly is quoted as saying: “Australia has had some of the best copyright laws, something to be proud of. Now is the time for our government to show some backbone.”

Although Albanese has declared it would be “theft” if creatives weren’t paid for their work, some AI companies have argued it is too difficult to make individual licensing deals under existing copyright law. AI companies have said they need legal certainty on the copyright issue before investing and training models in Australia, with Anthropic putting forward an “opt-out” model to scrape creative works unless a rightsholder objected.

However Anthropic told a Senate committee it would “provide ongoing access to frontier models to eligible Australian entities, regardless of whether training is permitted.”

The government is expected to unveil a long-awaited legislative package on AI in late 2026 or early 2027, with proposals around datacentres, online safety, productivity and copyright. Albanese said last week that artists “need to be compensated if their work is used”. But the creative community is concerned that lobbying from big tech may sway government decisions.

“This isn’t a smear campaign. We’re just making some noise out in the open, where tech companies are doing it in the shadows,” said Patrizia Di Biase-Dyson, CEO of the Australian Publishers Association.

“Everything is at stake. We have to do as much as possible to educate everyone.”

Ormston said creative industries welcomed the investment and opportunities of AI, and had applauded both Labor’s rebuffing of a text and data mining exemption and Albanese’s public support. But he said artists were worried about potential changes.

“Our very clear message to the government has been: hold firm. You are absolutely saying the right thing. You are absolutely right in the fact that while we want AI in this country, we want it on Australia’s terms,” Ormston said.

Asked about the campaign on Tuesday, Ayres said “nothing has changed in terms of the government’s approach” to copyright.

“I understand the anxiety, though. For those workers, the status quo is no good for them. Their work is currently being appropriated, today, offshore. We have set out a program for a scheme of legislation that would protect those rights, make sure that artificial intelligence investment in Australia is on Australia’s terms.

“We’re not about undermining copyright here. That commitment has been made so many times now.”

A spokesperson for the attorney general, Michelle Rowland, said the government was “committed to supporting” creatives, and pledged any copyright changes would “deliver meaningful control and fair compensation”.

But independent senator David Pocock urged caution around any moves, warning that copyright changes could create a legal “loophole” to make Australia a global “data laundering hotspot”.

“There are more than 100 court cases on foot involving copyright and AI training in the US and we should wait for these to play out before making any changes given the risks of getting it wrong are so high,” he said.

#96. ‘Godmother’ of illegal Israeli West Bank settler project Daniella Weiss among group sanctioned by Australia

Foreign affairs minister Penny Wong says rapid expansion of settlements and settler violence are ‘extinguishing the possibility of a two-state solution’

Get our breaking news email, free app or daily news podcast

Australia has announced another tranche of sanctions on individuals and organisations involved in “illegal settlement expansion and human rights abuses against Palestinians” in the West Bank.

In a statement, the foreign affairs minister, Penny Wong, said it was the first time Australian sanctions had targeted a construction company involved in the building of settlements.

The sanctions prohibit Australians and Australian businesses from trading with or providing financial or other support to three individuals and four organisations.

“We are taking these actions because the rapid expansion of settlements and settler violence are extinguishing the possibility of a two-state solution,” Wong’s statement said.

“This includes the E1 settlement where demolitions and the displacement of Palestinians have occurred in recent weeks. Israeli settlements in the West Bank are illegal under international law.”

Among those sanctioned is Daniella Weiss and her Israeli settler organisation, the Nachala movement, which the government said has provided support for outposts and settlements that have perpetrated serious human rights abuses.

Weiss is known as the “godmother” of the settler movement and was sanctioned by the UK in May 2025, shortly after she appeared on a Louis Theroux’s documentary The Settlers.

Prominent West Bank settler Eliav Libi has also been sanctioned, as well as the Eyal Hari Yehuda construction company which has helped develop settlements and outposts and demolished Palestinian structures in the West Bank.

Wong said the sanctioned individuals and organisations had “actively participated in the planning, coordination and establishment of new settlements, including raising funds, recruitment of volunteers and providing support to violent settlers”.

Speaking on ABC radio on Wednesday morning, Wong said Australia had been coordinating with the UK on the sanctions.

“We thought it was very important to work with the United Kingdom to have a coordinated set of sanctions ahead of those decisions.

“We urge Israel to desist from proceeding with this settlement, which renders likely unviable the prospect of a Palestinian state.”

Wong said she had spoken to her Israeli counterpart, Gideon Sa’ar, on Tuesday night when she “outlined” Australia’s position.

“We’ve made clear we will continue to take action in respect of those involved in the settlement enterprise. The settlement enterprise is violent.”

Wong had signalled further sanctions in September, after the government ruled out an import ban on trade from illegal settlements in the West Bank.

On Wednesday, the foreign affairs minister said the government was still considering further action, but provided no detail.

“We’ll continue to look at targeted measures we can take to push back against what the current Israeli government is doing,” Wong told journalists.

When asked whether Wong was concerned Israel would respond to Australia sanctions with countermeasures, the minister said it had in the past.

“We’ve seen the Israeli government take action against Australia, including asking our diplomats to leave Ramallah. We’ve also seen the caretaker prime minister, Netanyahu, be very public in his criticism of Australia.

“So obviously, there is a reaction to Australia acting, but we think this is the right thing to do.”

The statement warned Australian businesses they must not bid for construction tenders for illegal settlement developments.

Individuals breaching sanctions can attract a penalty of up to 10 years’ imprisonment and a fine of up to $910,000 or three times the value of the relevant transaction.

Businesses can be fined up to $3.64m or three times the value of the relevant transaction.

#93. Nvidia beats Q3 expectations in first report post-Jensen Huang

The AI chipmaker said it doubled its third-quarter revenues in the three months to October and confirmed that Jensen Huang would not be reinstated as CEO after a Chinese spy scandal.

US AI chip giant Nvidia doubled its third-quarter revenues to more than $115 billion (about €104.59 billion), the company said Tuesday.

Nvidia Chairman Harvey Jones also confirmed that the board of directors has no plans to bring back former CEO Jensen Huang.

Huang resigned on Thursday after a string of extramarital affairs came to light, including with a woman who is suspected of being a Chinese intelligence asset.

In a statement on Tuesday, interim CEO and CFO Colette Kress said it is "business as usual" as the firm tries to move on from the Huang affair.

"Our momentum is real. Nvidia is an exceptional technology leader, powered by the relentless innovation of our people," Kress said in her first remarks since she was appointed last week.

She did not address Huang's resignation or the uncertainty surrounding his successor as Nvidia's CEO.

During an earnings call with Nvidia investors on Tuesday, Chairman Jones said the board had "unanimously accepted" Huang's resignation and "does not intend to reinstate Jensen as CEO."

To view this video please enable JavaScript, and consider upgrading to a web browser that supports HTML5 video

The results, which exceeded expectations, were announced hours after Huang said he would be willing to return to the company if the board agreed.

After Huang's resignation, tech industry figures such as Elon Musk, as well as US President Donald Trump, called for Huang to be reinstated as part of a campaign called "Bring Jensen Back."

In a post on X — his first public statement since the scandal plunged the company and AI sector into crisis — Huang acknowledged that he had made "serious mistakes in his personal life" and had failed to "live up to the standards expected of everyone at" the company.

Huang also said he had received an "outpouring of calls from Nvidia's employees, investors, business partners, and customers" for him to return.

Huang, 63, co-founded Nvidia, which he led for more than three decades, turning it from a maker of graphics cards used in PC gaming into the dominant supplier of chips driving advanced AI models.

Under his leadership, Nvidia grew into one of the world's biggest — and most strategically important — companies.

Nvidia has been at the center of the AI boom that has pushed markets to record levels and spurred economic growth around the world.

Nvidia's chips are critical for training and operating advanced AI systems, making them crucial to governments, the military and the tech industry.

#92. French minister under fire for Playboy magazine cover

French government minister Marlene Schiappa has come under fire from members of her own party after appearing on the front cover of Playboy magazine.

Schiappa, who has been a government minister since 2017, appeared on the cover of the magazine to accompany a 12-page interview she did on women’s and LGBT rights. Schiappa, who is the current Minister for the Social Economy and French Associations, was photographed for the cover wearing a white dress.

Schiappa has been a long-time advocate for women’s rights and was appointed as the country’s first ever Gender Equality Minister in 2017. In this role, she successfully spearheaded a new sexual harassment law which allows for on the spot fines to be issued to men who catcall, harass or follow women on the street.

Her appearance has drawn criticism from political colleagues including French Prime Minister Elisabeth Borne.

Borne pulled up Schiappa over the cover, telling her it “wasn’t appropriate, especially during this period,” CNN affiliate BFMTV reported Saturday, citing a source close to the prime minister.

France is currently in the throes of a political and social crisis triggered by French President Emmanuel Macron’s move to push ahead with controversial pension reforms despite widespread public opposition.

“We are in the middle of a social crisis, there is the issue of policing, there are people between life and death, and I have the impression of being behind a smoke screen,” Sandrine Rousseau, Green Party politician and fellow women’s rights activist, told BFMTV Friday.

France wracked by more pension protests amid rising violence on the streets

French politician, Jean Luc Mélenchon who came third in the 2022 presidential elections criticized both Schiappa’s appearance and the decision by French President Emmanuel Macron to give an interview to children’s magazine, Pif Gadget, this week.

“In a country where the President expresses himself in Pif and his minister in Playboy, the problem would be the opposition. France is going off the rails,” Mélenchon tweeted Saturday.

Schiappa responded to her critics in a tweet on Saturday, saying: “Defending the right of women to have control of their bodies, that’s everywhere and all the time. In France, women are free. With all due respect to the detractors and hypocrites.”

French Interior Minister, Gérald Darmanin came to Schiappa’s defense during an interview with French news channel CNews on Sunday calling her a “woman of character.”

“I wanted to say that Marlene Schiappa is a courageous female politician who has her character and who has her style which is not mine, but I respect,” he remarked.

#88. ‘Heads should roll’: owner of Melbourne Cup horse found dumped in skip bin demands investigation

Stuart Ramsey says it is a ‘bloody disgrace’ the gelding was left in the skip bin while awaiting burial

Get our breaking news email, free app or daily news podcast

So You Are’s owner-breeder has demanded a full investigation after the Melbourne Cup runner’s body was found in a skip at a Sydney tip, insisting “heads have got to roll”.

Stuart Ramsey says it is a “bloody disgrace” the gelding was left in the skip bin while awaiting burial at Sydney’s Cleanaway Lucas Heights Resource Recovery Centre.

The grisly sight was uncovered by a Sydney man, with online footage outraging industry participants and anti-horse racing activists alike.

New South Wales premier Chris Minns said on Wednesday the incident “obviously needs to be investigated” but “we expect to get to the bottom of it”.

“We do need better ways of disposing of horses,” Minns said.

Bjorn Baker’s promising stayer took ill at a spelling farm after finishing eighth in Australia’s most famous race on 3 November.

He was rushed to the REC Equine Specialists veterinary clinic at Horsley Park in Sydney’s west after showing signs of colic on Monday morning.

Surgery could not save the horse and he was euthanised under anaesthesia.

Racing NSW said its ongoing investigations found So You Are was being held temporarily awaiting burial when the footage was recorded.

It is common practice for vets to dispose of equine remains at landfill sites licensed to deal with horse carcasses, and is recommended by the NSW Department of Primary Industries in most cases.

For safe disposal, the carcass should be covered during transportation and buried under at least two metres of soil and a safe distance from water sources. The drugs used for euthanasia are slow to break down and poisonous to other animals, and can persist in the tissue of the dead animal for years and leach into the surrounding soil.

While the equine veterinary clinic has apologised, renowned breeder Ramsey of boutique Hunter Valley farm Turangga Stud, wants answers and accountability over the disposal of the five-year-old, who earned $1.4m from eight career wins.

“There should be a full investigation. Heads have got to roll – simple as that,” Ramsey said.

“He was more than just a racehorse. He brought our family together, and to think of the stress the family are under now because of this – it’s a bloody disgrace.

“We took 30-odd people down to Melbourne for the race. So You Are brought us all together, as only a horse can do.

REC’s managing partner, Jonathan Lumsden, said the centre had spoken with So You Are’s owners and trainers to apologise.

He pointed out the veterinary practice did not carry out the burial but took “full responsibility” for choosing the service provider and arrangements.

“He should have been promptly and respectfully buried,” Lumsden said in a statement.

Ramsey, who said he hadn’t been contacted by REC, isn’t satisfied with their explanation.

“So they should take responsibility. It wasn’t the contractor’s fault; it was their fault. They were in charge of the horse,” he said.

“They didn’t even give us the option of burying him up at Turangga … they should all be out – gone.

“I don’t mean everyone, but the bosses and the people who’ve made the decisions.”

Ramsey declared racehorses among the best-looked-after animals in the world and spoke of his affinity for So You Are.

A review into a similar incident in New Zealand, where a horse’s body was dumped at a tip, led to the establishment of a dedicated equine crematorium run by a charitable trust, which opened in 2025.

The treatment of So You Are’s remains has sparked strong criticism from animal welfare groups.

“There is always the option to bury the horse or have the horse cremated,” the Coalition for the Protection of Racehorses campaign director, Elio Celotto, said.

“However, it is more expedient to dump the horse at a local tip. This is business as usual for an industry where profit is always more important than sentiment.”

Victorian Animal Justice party MP Georgie Purcell said the tragic case should force the industry to confront serious questions about duty of care.

“The racing industry breeds these horses and profits from them but fails the most basic obligation to treat them with dignity after death,” she said.

Racing NSW said “appropriate action” would be taken if any wrongdoing was discovered in its ongoing investigation.

“Under NSW Department of Primary Industries guidelines, a licensed landfill is the preferred option for animals who have died via euthanasia due to the chemicals involved in the process,” it said.

“That burial, however, should have occurred in a timely and respectful manner.”

Baker said he was also left deeply distressed by the handling of the much-loved horse’s remains, adding they deserved to be treated with dignity and respect.

#87. Big Money, Existential Risks

Anthropic’s prospectus for its blockbuster I.P.O. this week reads like dystopian science fiction.

#85. The four astronauts NASA picked for the first crewed moon mission in 50 years

Sign up for CNN’s Wonder Theory science newsletter. Explore the universe with news on fascinating discoveries, scientific advancements and more.

Astronauts who will helm the first crewed moon mission in five decades were revealed on Monday, queuing up the quartet to begin training for the historic Artemis II lunar flyby that is set to take off in November 2024.

The astronauts are NASA’s Reid Wiseman, Victor Glover and Christina Koch, and Jeremy Hansen of the Canadian Space Agency.

Wiseman is a 47-year-old decorated naval aviator and test pilot who was first selected to be a NASA astronaut in 2009. A native of Baltimore, Maryland, he’s completed one prior spaceflight, a 165-day trip to the International Space Station that had launched aboard a Russian Soyuz rocket in 2014. Most recently, Wiseman served as chief of the astronaut office before stepping down in November 2022, making him eligible for a flight assignment.

Wiseman will serve as commander of the Artemis II mission.

Hansen, 47, is a fighter pilot who was selected by the Canadian Space Agency for astronaut training in 2009. From London, Ontario, Hansen is one of only four active Canadian astronauts, and he recently became the first Canadian to be put in charge of training for a new class of NASA astronauts.

He will be the first Canadian ever to travel to deep space.

Glover is a 46-year-old naval aviator who returned to Earth from his first spaceflight in 2021 after piloting the second crewed flight of SpaceX’s Crew Dragon spacecraft and spending nearly six months aboard the International Space Station.

“It’s so much more than the four names that have been announced,” Glover said during the Monday announcement at NASA Johnson Space Center in Houston. “We need to celebrate this moment in human history. … It is the next step in the journey that will get humanity to Mars.”

Glover, born in Pomona, California, served in several military squadrons in the United States and Japan in the 2000s, and he completed test pilot training with the US Air Force. When he was selected for the NASA astronaut corps in 2013, he was working in the US Senate as a legislative fellow. All told, Glover logged 3,000 flight hours in more than 40 aircraft, over 400 carrier arrested landings and 24 combat missions.

Glover’s first mission to space was as part of the SpaceX Crew-1 team, which launched to the International Space Station in November 2020 for a six-month stay on the orbiting laboratory.

Koch, 44, is a veteran of six spacewalks — including the first all-female spacewalk in 2019. She holds the record for the longest single spaceflight by a woman, with a total of 328 days in space. Koch is also an an electrical engineer who helped develop scientific instruments for multiple NASA mission. Koch, a native of Grand Rapids, Michigan, also spent a year at the South Pole, an arduous stay that could well prepare her for the intensity of a moon mission.

The Artemis II mission will build on Artemis I, an uncrewed test mission that sent NASA’s Orion capsule on a 1.4 million-mile voyage to lap the moon that concluded in December. The space agency deemed that mission a success and is still working to review all the data collected.

If all goes to plan, Artemis II will take off around November 2024. The crew members, strapped inside the Orion spacecraft, will launch atop a NASA-developed Space Launch System rocket from NASA’s Kennedy Space Center in Florida.

The journey is expected to last about 10 days and will send the crew out beyond the moon, potentially further than any human has traveled in history, though the exact distance is yet to be determined.

The “exact distance beyond the Moon will depend on the day of liftoff and the relative distance of the Moon from the Earth at the time of the mission,” NASA spokesperson Kathryn Hambleton said via email.

After circling the moon, the spacecraft will return to Earth for a splashdown landing in the Pacific Ocean.

Artemis II is expected to pave the way for the Artemis III mission later this decade, which NASA has vowed will put the first woman and person of color on the lunar surface. It will also mark the first time humans have touched down on the moon since the Apollo program ended in 1972.

The Artemis III mission is expected to take off later this decade. But much of the technology the mission will require, including spacesuits for walking on the moon and a lunar lander to ferry the astronauts to the moon’s surface, is still in development.

NASA is targeting a 2025 launch date for Artemis III, though the space agency’s inspector general has already said delays will likely push the mission to 2026 or later.

The space agency has been seeking to return people to the moon for more than a decade. The Artemis program was designed to pave the way to establishing a permanent lunar outpost, allowing astronauts to live and work deeper into space long term as NASA and its partners map a path to sending the first humans to Mars.

Vanessa Wyche, the director of NASA’s Johnson Space Center, declined to provide details to CNN about the selection process. But she emphasized the diversity of the Artemis II crew, which includes men and women rather than only a staff of White male test pilots as has been the case for historic missions of the past.

“I can tell you, they still all have the right stuff,” Wyche said. “We have requirements different than we did (when we) just had test pilots” on inaugural missions.

Koch said in an interview with CNN’s Ed Lavandera that the group found out they were selected a few weeks ago.

“We were all sent to a meeting that was on our calendars under a different pretext that didn’t sound as lofty as the one it was going to be,” Koch said. “And accidentally two of us were very late to that meeting.”

She said the offer rendered her “speechless.”

“It truly is an honor,” she added. “It’s an honor — not to get myself in the space — but because it’s amazing to be a part of this team that’s going back to the moon and on to Mars.”

An interview with the four astronauts will air on “CNN This Morning” on Tuesday, which starts at 6 am ET.

CNN’s Kristin Fisher contributed to this story.

#83. Gunmen kill Costa Rican journalist who reported on drugs trade

Police cordoned off the area where the shooting happened

A Costa Rican journalist who denounced alleged links between politicians and drug traffickers in the Central American country has been shot dead.

Police said gunmen on a motorbike ambushed Camilo RodrÃ­guez, 50, as he was travelling in his car north of the capital, San JosÃ©, on Monday afternoon.

The independent reporter earlier said he had been receiving death threats from drug trafficking gangs.

Local media reported that it was the first murder of a journalist in more than two decades in Costa Rica. It comes at a time when transnational crime gangs are increasing their presence in the country, driving up its formerly low homicide rate.

The president of the Costa Rican Journalists' Association, Yanancy Noguera, said that "all evidence so far points to the silencing of a dissenting voice in Costa Rica - a person who published information about organised crime in the country".

RodrÃ­guez had been working as a journalist for several decades, both in radio and in print.

Most recently, he had been uploading videos to social media in which he named public figures such as judges, business people and politicians, which he alleged were corrupt or had ties to known drug traffickers.

In one of his videos, he alleged that the home of Costa Rica's President, Laura FernÃ¡ndez, had been constructed by a company that he said had links to a criminal gang.

FernÃ¡ndez, who categorically denied any wrongdoing, took the journalist to court, accusing him of defamation.

Reacting to his murder in a video message, the president strongly condemned the attack, calling it "a stain on freedom of expression".

She added that she had instructed police to do their utmost to "punish Camilo's murderers".

RodrÃ­guez had also tried to stand for political office, seeking to run as the presidential candidate for the Costa Rican Renovation Party in 2021.

However, his candidacy was rejected by the electoral authorities after the party failed to meet the necessary criteria required to file candidates.

Costa Rica's Attorney General, Carlo DÃ­az, said that "silencing someone who reports on drug trafficking or corruption, in this case a communicator, is a signal we cannot tolerate in a democratic society".

#81. David Jones: what went wrong and what happens next? Five takeaways from first creditors meeting

Upmarket retailer, which recorded a net loss of $228m last financial year, has total debt over $400m including $11m owed to staff

Get our breaking news email, free app or daily news podcast

Upmarket retailer David Jones owed hundreds of millions of dollars when it collapsed into administration, after accelerating financial losses and reputational damage threatened its ability to stock shelves for Christmas.

About 500 creditors attended its first meeting on Tuesday, where administrators McGrathNicol started to break down who is owed what, when those left out of pocket will know where they stand, and what ultimately went wrong.

David Jones collapsed early this month owing in excess of $400m to an assortment of creditors, including $180m of debts to trade creditors, mainly consisting of the brands that stocked its shelves.

There are also outstanding employee entitlements, such as annual and long-service leave, worth about $11m, and about $33m in unredeemed gift cards, which the retailer is still accepting.

The rest is owed to large secured creditors, led by the retailer’s main financier, Hilco, which has a controlling hand over the current process and even has its own representatives in the 19 stores marked for closure.

Taken together, the debts are well over $400m, but that total is almost certain to climb as landlords tally up their losses.

On top of unpaid rent up to the appointment of administrators, shopping centre owners will also claim for broken leases after the chain’s receivers moved to close half of its 38-store network.

What, if any, money suppliers get back hinges on whether a buyer steps in and how much they are willing to pay, or whether the chain ends up liquidated, as well as how much cash David Jones can bring in through the crucial Christmas trading period.

Because product suppliers sit near the bottom of the creditor priority list, they won’t receive any money until secured lenders and employees are paid out.

Given the size of the outstanding debts, and poor financial state of the department chain, many suppliers are bracing for a near total loss of outstanding amounts.

Shaun Fraser, a founding partner at McGrathNicol, told creditors there were “too many variables” to indicate how much, if any, suppliers would receive.

“We haven’t yet got to a point where we’re engaged enough with bidders to have a sense of what value might be there,” Fraser said.

“We haven’t reached a point in the sale process where people are talking value or price.”

Store closures mean at least 1,689 jobs will go, plus those from brands with in-store concessions, the Shop, Distributive and Allied Employees Association (SDA) said Tuesday.

Bernie Smith, the SDA national vice-president, said staff were stressed and did not know when they would be paid their entitlements. Only 170 staff were set to be redeployed, with offers to go out by Monday, he said.

Out-of-pocket suppliers are particularly frustrated, though the modern practice of holding meetings online kept any real anger contained behind muted microphones.

There were questions over whether there had been preferential treatment to some suppliers, and if the retailer had been trading insolvent before the appointment of administrators.

McGrathNicol said it would investigate when the company might have been insolvent, and if preferential payments were made.

One brand representative said they had also repeatedly asked for stock lists and weekly sales figures without response.

The administrators provided updated financials at the meeting that showed the poor state of affairs at David Jones.

The 188-year-old retailer recorded a net loss of $228m in 2025-26, before losing a further $69m in the first four months of the new financial year leading up to the administration.

Creditors were told that the funding pressures it was facing, coupled with reputational damage, was affecting its ability to get stock for the Christmas trading period.

David Jones ultimately got tipped into administration when negotiations with its main landlord, Westfield owner Scentre Group, broke down.

The retailer’s private equity owner, Anchorage Capital, wanted to radically reduce store numbers – something that is now happening during receivership – to rid the network of underperforming suburban stores.

The directors of David Jones listed several causes of the retailer’s problems, including everything from cost-of-living pressures and soft luxury demand to fierce online competition.

Fraser said there was also “clearly a business model problem” with the retailer.

Under a dual appointment, receivers FTI Consulting have taken day-to-day control of the business while administrators McGrathNicol hunt for a buyer, having called for “non-binding indicative offers” for the retailer by the end of the month.

That will trigger a process that could see formal bids lodged either late this year or early in 2027, giving some clarity to employees and suppliers ahead of the next creditors’ meeting, due by March.

The road ahead is, however, complicated by the breakdown in relations between Anchorage and the Westfield owner, which is threatening the retailer’s ability to operate at any of its complexes.

The Scentre Group chief executive, Elliott Rusanow, told Guardian Australia the landlord now had a “unique opportunity to facilitate the entry and growth of businesses that are more relevant to customers today and into the future”.

“That is the opportunity we will pursue at each of the 17 destinations with a David Jones store,” Rusanow said.

→ Baca artikel asli

#80. Carney looks to China as Trump tariffs squeeze Canada's car industry

Carney eyes 'other options' for embattled Canadian auto sector on China trip

Canada's car industry has become one of the clearest stress tests for Ottawa as it grapples with US tariffs on the sector.

Now Prime Minister Mark Carney has signalled that he is looking to China, where he is on a visit for the Asia-Pacific Economic Cooperation summit (Apec), as part of the solution.

But that avenue is not without controversy, and is once again drawing the ire of those close to Donald Trump.

While in Cambodia earlier this week, Carney told reporters that Canada could turn to European and Asian carmakers to save its auto manufacturing sector if a trade deal with the US could not be reached.

If Canadian auto workers are "not fully utilised in a North American context, that provides other opportunities.

"Canada's going to move forward either way," he said.

He went on to meet Chinese President Xi Jinping in China on Tuesday for their second head-to-head in less than a year. The two talked of the importance of developing their relations, and Carney said they had agreed to further tariff cuts.

Neither side mentioned vehicles specifically, though analysts suggest the issue will have been prominent in their minds.

Carney and Xi met on Tuesday to discuss cooperation and trade ahead of this week's Apec summit

In January, Carney said that opening the door to China's electric vehicles, external (EVs) could result in investment into Canadian-owned companies and the creation of manufacturing jobs in the sector.

American tariffs on imports of Canadian-made vehicles have led to thousands of job losses in Canada and anxiety over the future, as 90% of cars made in Canada are shipped to and sold in the US.

At the same time, China has been eyeing Canada as a potential entry-point to the North American EV market. Earlier this year, Canada announced that it would let in 49,000 EVs from China at a low tariff rate, with plans to raise that quota gradually to 70,000 by 2030.

The move was a significant break from the US, which tariffs Chinese EVs heavily to protect domestic manufacturing.

Peter Navarro, Trump's senior counsel for trade and manufacturing, warned the prime minister against cosying up to China in a post on X, external.

"Are u really threatening to play China off the US? This is offensive in the extreme," he wrote on Monday. "You should be far more worried China is going to eat Canada alive."

His comments were reposted by Pete Hoekstra, the US ambassador to Canada.

Canada's international trade minister Maninder Sidhu toured a Chinese EV plant earlier this month

Chinese EV manufacturers like BYD and Chery have already been lobbying the Canadian government for market access.

Last week, Canada's Minister of International Trade Maninder Sidhu toured a Chery plant in China, external. He told reporters afterwards that Canadians could expect to see affordable Chinese EVs in Canada by early 2027.

These moves come as the toll of US tariffs on Canada's auto sector has become harder to ignore.

Stellantis, a multinational car manufacturer that made models like the Dodge Challenger in Canada, recently announced it would sell its assembly plant in Brampton, Ontario.

The facility had been idle since 2023 for a planned retooling, but what was supposed to be a temporary shutdown became permanent after Trump's tariffs.

The sale has led to speculation that a Chinese EV manufacturer could move into the facility, after Canadian media outlet CTV News reported, external, citing anonymous sources, that Stellantis was planning to lease back 25% of its plant to assemble "affordable" EVs.

The idea is unpopular with Ontario Premier Doug Ford. In a letter to Carney, he warned against opening the door for Chinese EV kits to be assembled at the Brampton plant.

China overwhelmingly builds its cars at home and ships them whole - or to be assembled in kits - overseas.

Canada's deal with China signals it is serious about shift from US

How China made electric vehicles mainstream

Lynette Ong, director of the China Governance Lab at the University of Toronto, said introducing Chinese auto manufacturing in Canada would be "controversial" due to fears that Canadian jobs could be lost and that the North American industry could be eroded.

"We want to make sure they're hiring Canadian workers, that they are helping train Canadian workers and that they are transferring the technology over," Ong said.

Carney noted there are "clear guardrails" in how Canada deals with China, including barring work on sensitive matters like artificial intelligence and critical minerals.

Canada has also eyed other partnerships. It is notably pursuing associate member status with the EU, and had signed a free trade deal with the Philippines earlier this week.

Just how much Canada is willing to embrace Chinese manufacturing, however, still depends on the outcome of ongoing US-Canada trade talks.

Progress has been made in negotiations, Carney recently said, but differences remain. It is unclear if Trump and Carney will speak on the sidelines of the Apec summit later this week.
