#74. Hugh Jackman, Rebel Wilson and 150 Australian artists join call to block Woodside gas development near Scott Reef

Actors, authors and musicians sign open letter urging Murray Watt to reject the fossil fuel company’s plan to drill for gas in Browse basin off Kimberley coast

Sign up for climate and environment editor Adam Morton’s free Clear Air newsletter here

More than 150 Australian artists, including Hugh Jackman, Rebel Wilson and Jessica Mauboy, have backed a Greenpeace-led campaign against Woodside Energy’s proposal to develop the Browse basin, about 300km off the Kimberley coast.

Woodside, the country’s largest fossil fuel company, describes Browse as “Australia’s biggest undeveloped offshore gas resource”. The oil and gas giant is hoping to win federal government approval to drill more than 50 gas wells about 3km from the Scott Reef area, which is home to more than 1,500 species, including endangered pygmy blue whales.

Actors Jackman, Wilson, Richard Roxburgh and Mia Wasikowska are among prominent Australians who oppose the Browse development on the basis that the proposed gasfield could damage the unique reef ecosystem.

They join musicians Jimmy Barnes, Paul Kelly, Tame Impala’s Kevin Parker, Tina Arena, Jessica Mauboy, G Flip, Vance Joy, Missy Higgins and Baker Boy in signing an open letter calling on the federal environment minister, Murray Watt, not to approve the development.

The letter – which says the Browse project “would turn Scott Reef into an industrial gas zone with decades of deafening seismic blasting [and] carbon dumping beneath the ocean floor” – was also signed by authors JM Coetzee, Helen Garner, Anna Funder and Diana Reid.

Sign up to get climate and environment editor Adam Morton’s Clear Air column as a free newsletter

“Drilling at Scott Reef could cause an oil spill up to 30 times bigger than the 2009 Montara disaster, polluting Scott Reef with oil condensate, impacting the Kimberley coastline and even reaching as far as Indonesia,” it continued.

The open letter comes after a supergroup of more than 20 Australian artists – including King Gizzard & the Lizard Wizard, Barnes, John Butler and Montaigne – last Monday released If Not Now, a musical call to arms about the reef.

The song, a reimagining of King Gizzard’s 2020 track, If Not Now, Then When?, is a collaboration between Greenpeace Australia Pacific, Green Music Australia and p(doom) Records.

Browse, first proposed by Woodside in 2018, would see gas piped about 900km to its ageing North West Shelf gas export plant, which is adjacent to the world heritage-listed Murujuga rock art.

Last month, Guardian Australia revealed that WA’s independent environmental watchdog had sharply criticised the proposal, saying that plans to protect turtles and blue whales near Scott Reef would be ineffective.

A draft letter from the Environmental Protection Authority, written in 2023, advised that it may be impossible to reduce the risk of an oil spill to an acceptable level.

Greenpeace last month nominated Scott Reef for national heritage listing, based on its ecological and scientific values.

Modelling by the Institute for Energy Economics and Financial Analysis suggests that Browse would generate up to 6.8m tonnes of carbon dioxide emissions yearly at peak production, accounting for between 2.9% and 3.7% of Australia’s emissions in 2035.

“Browse gas is likely four times more expensive than existing domestic gas,” the institute found last year, adding that the project “is unlikely to be competitive either domestically or internationally, based on IEEFA cost estimates”.

Matt Roberts, executive director of the Conservation Council of Western Australia, earlier this month described Woodside’s proposal as “extremely concerning”.

“At the end of the day, we don’t need Browse gas. Western Australia has enough gas available to support us through the transition to a renewable future and regional demand for LNG is declining.

“The federal and state governments have enough evidence to reject this disastrous proposal and save Scott Reef, and we expect them to do just that.”

A spokesperson for the national EPA has told Guardian Australia the development was being assessed under the national Environment Protection and Biodiversity Conservation Act, and the “decision clock” had been paused while the agency waited on “further technical advice”.

A Woodside spokesperson said the proposed development “remains subject to a detailed, public and transparent assessment by state and Commonwealth regulators”.

Feedback had helped reduce “the infrastructure footprint around environmentally sensitive areas like Scott Reef – which will not be built on, drilled or mined”, they said.

“Browse is a natural gas and condensate development, not an oil development. References to a potential ‘oil spill’ from Browse are inaccurate.” The spokesperson added that the “longest credible duration of a potential well blowout, while still highly unlikely”, was around 12 hours.

#73. UK sanctions firms over Israeli West Bank settlements

The E1 area east of Jerusalem is a strategically important part of the occupied West Bank

The UK has sanctioned companies it said are involved in building settlements in the occupied West Bank, in the first package of measures since the government announced a tougher approach to Israeli actions in the territory.

The government said the sanctions against eight construction and property development firms would disrupt their flows of finance and ability to support building in the West Bank.

The construction of settlements, which are illegal under international law, has risen sharply in recent years. Settler attacks on Palestinians and their property have also surged.

Israeli Foreign Minister Gideon Saar called the sanctions a "hostile act targeting private, law-abiding companies".

Criticising the move on X, he wrote that Israel - as it has done in the past - will respond with "countermeasures", and that "decisions will be announced in due course".

"Israel will not bow to external pressure or change its policies as a result," Saar said, adding that they will "consider new ways to strengthen Jewish communities" in the West Bank, and "protect those involved in developing and building".

Israel previously condemned UK sanctions related to West Bank settlements, accusing the Labour government of "systematically acting against the State of Israel".

The Palestinian foreign ministry welcomed the sanctions and called on other countries to follow suit.

It comes two days before the deadline for bids for construction tenders in the highly sensitive E1 area east of Jerusalem.

The plans, which have been condemned internationally, would in effect divide the West Bank in two and cut it off from East Jerusalem.

UK Foreign Secretary Ed Miliband has said the E1 development would render a two-state solution "unviable" by "cutting through the heart of Palestine".

The sanctions also target Tzav 9, an Israeli activist movement which the UK government says has organised and carried out blockades to prevent aid entering Gaza.

Responding to the announcement, the group said: "Tzav 9 is a movement of abiding citizens from all the political spectrum, and we've always protested peacefully."

It comes after the UK expanded its human rights sanctions regime last month, enabling the government to sanction individuals and companies involved in violations of international humanitarian law anywhere in the world.

The government is also developing separate legislation to ban the import of goods from Israeli settlements and the provision of advertising services for settlement properties in the UK.

Miliband said: "Protecting the two-state solution is the North Star of this government's approach.

"That is why my message on E1 is clear: do not bid, do not build, and do not profit from the destruction of the two-state solution.

"The status quo in the West Bank and Gaza is unacceptable.

"We are committed to working productively with a new Israeli government and the Palestinian Authority to advance lasting peace and security for Israelis and Palestinians alike."

In September, the UK government announced that it would ban the import of goods from Israeli settlements, as well as acting against companies and individuals providing services for settlement expansion.

At the time, Miliband said the move marked the beginning of a "new approach" to the Israeli-Palestinian conflict, which "not only calls out injustice and suffering, but acts".

That announcement prompted a furious reaction from Israel, which said it would shut the British consulate in East Jerusalem in retaliation.

Last month, most diplomats left the consulate - which handled UK relations with Palestinians in Jerusalem as well as in the West Bank and Gaza - but the UK insisted that the building would not be closing and would operate as a "UK mission".

Israel has built about 160 settlements housing 700,000 Jews since it occupied the West Bank and East Jerusalem during the 1967 Middle East war. An estimated 3.3 million Palestinians live alongside them.

There has been a surge in attacks by settlers on Palestinians and their property in the West Bank since the start of the Gaza war, which was triggered by the Hamas-led attack on Israel on 7 October 2023.

#71. Beijing promised to 'fight back' over Taiwan leader's US visit. But this time it has more to lose

An anticipated meeting between Taiwan’s President Tsai Ing-wen and US House Speaker Kevin McCarthy in California this week has sparked concerns of a repeat of the pressure campaign China launched last year when then-Speaker Nancy Pelosi visited Taipei.

At that time, Beijing encircled the island democracy with unprecedented military drills – firing multiple missiles into its surrounding waters and sending dozens of warplanes speeding across a sensitive median line dividing the Taiwan Strait.

It also cut off contact with the United States over a number of issues from military matters to combating climate change, in retaliation for what it viewed as a violation of its sovereignty.

This time, Beijing has already threatened to “resolutely fight back” if a Tsai-McCarthy meeting goes ahead.

It also slammed Washington for allowing Tsai to stopover in the US while en route to and from official visits in Central America, warning it could lead to “serious” confrontation between the two powers.

A defiant Tsai staked out her own ground, pledging as she took off on her 10-day trip not to let “external pressure” stop Taiwan from connecting with the world and like-minded democracies.

But the optics of the meeting, taking place in California and not Taiwan, and its timing – at a particularly thorny moment in China’s foreign relations and ahead of a presidential election in Taiwan that could reset the tone of its relationship with Beijing – may see Beijing tread more carefully this time, or at least not escalate further, analysts say.

“This puts the burden on China not to overreact, because any overreaction is only going to push China further away from the world,” said Yun Sun, director of the China Program at the Stimson Center think tank in Washington.

That doesn’t mean, however, that Beijing won’t be closely watching Tsai’s movements as it calibrates its response – and decides how much military might to flex over her meeting with an American lawmaker on American soil.

The opacity of China’s system – and the potential for competing interests within its vast bureaucracy – also make it difficult to accurately predict its response.

“Every time Taiwan does anything that China doesn’t like, the Chinese react with their own military coercion,” Sun said. But in the current situation, “they have to consider the consequences of overreaction,” she added.

The expected meeting, which McCarthy’s office announced earlier this week would take place on Wednesday, also comes at a precarious moment in US-China relations.

Washington and Beijing are struggling to stabilize their communication amid flaring tensions over issues from a downed suspected Chinese surveillance balloon to semiconductor supply chains – raising the stakes of potential damage to that relationship if Beijing lashes out as it did when Tsai met Pelosi.

Taiwan is still feeling the fallout of that response last August, with Chinese military forces now regularly making incursions over what had previously been an informal but largely respected border of control between Beijing and Taipei in the Taiwan Strait. Taiwan’s official Central News Agency also reported Monday that Tsai would meet with McCarthy, citing Tsai’s presidential office.

But a meeting between Tsai and the leader of the Republican majority in the US House of Representatives, who ranks second in line to the Presidency, would mark another symbolic moment for Taiwan and the US, which only maintain unofficial ties.

For Tsai, who is entering the final year of her two-term presidency, “it’s clearly a capstone event,” according to Wen-Ti Sung, a political scientist at the Australian National University’s Taiwan Studies Program. “She has this image as the Taiwanese president who has taken US-Taiwan relations to new heights, and who … has been able to give Taiwan almost unprecedented international visibility,” he said.

That increased visibility – and enhanced cooperation with the US – has followed China’s mounting pressure on the island, which sits fewer than 110 miles (177 kilometers) from the mainland coast.

China’s Communist Party claims the self-governing island democracy as its own despite never having controlled it, and has vowed to take the island, by force if necessary.

China thinks it's diplomatically isolating Taiwan. It isn't

The party has undertaken a sweeping expansion of its military capabilities over the past decade under leader Xi Jinping – and ramped up its pervasive economic, diplomatic and military pressure on Taiwan.

That’s driven concerns, among some in Washington, that Beijing is preparing for an invasion, though China’s official language still suggests that scenario is not its preferred option for achieving the claimed goal of “reunification.”

It is those pressures – and how to support Taiwan against unilateral actions by Beijing – that are likely to be on the table when Tsai, McCarthy and a bipartisan group of US lawmakers sit down on Wednesday.

Congress has been a pillar of increasing American support for Taiwan in recent years. Lawmakers regularly visit the island and drive bipartisan legislation enhancing support and cooperation.

While the US switched its diplomatic relations to Beijing decades ago, it maintains unofficial ties with Taiwan and is bound by law to provide the democratic island with the means to defend itself.

Under Washington’s longstanding “One China” policy, the US acknowledges China’s position that Taiwan is part of China, but has never officially recognized Beijing’s claim to the island of 23 million.

Though McCarthy does not have Pelosi’s decades-long record of advocacy regarding China, the California Republican is now a leading voice pushing for closer scrutiny of Beijing, and meeting Tsai could help him to burnish that image.

Last month, McCarthy told reporters that meeting Tsai in the US would not impact whether he travels to Taiwan in the future – something he had earlier said he wanted to do.

A meeting in California, on US soil, is widely seen as less likely to provoke Beijing than a McCarthy visit to Taiwan.

Pelosi’s trip – the first from a lawmaker of that rank to the island in 25 years – generated a fever pitch of nationalist and anti-US rhetoric in mainland China.

This time, so far, domestic conversation in China’s heavily controlled media sphere has been significantly muted.

But the stakes remain high – including for Beijing itself – over how it responds, analysts say.

As Taiwan prepares for a presidential election in January, a fierce response could push voters away from Taiwan’s main opposition party Kuomintang (KMT), widely seen as more friendly toward Beijing.

It could also jar with another high profile trip happening now: a tour of mainland China from former Taiwan president and senior KMT member Ma Ying-jeou, the first visit from a current or former Taiwan leader since the end of the Chinese Civil War in 1949.

Ma’s tour is a “once in a half a century opportunity to send a conciliatory message between the two sides, Beijing shouldn’t want to tank that,” said Sung, the political scientist.

What you need to know about China-Taiwan tensions

China is also acutely aware that its actions toward Taiwan are under a significantly brighter global spotlight following the invasion of Ukraine by Russian President Vladimir Putin, a close diplomatic partner of Xi. Putin’s rhetoric over Ukraine has echoes of how Xi speaks of Taiwan.

Beijing has recently sought to position itself as an agent of peace in that conflict – especially as it aims to repair frayed ties with Europe.

This week, as Tsai is expected to meet with McCarthy, French President Emmanuel Macron and European Commission President Ursula von der Leyen will head to China – an important opportunity that Xi may not want to overshadow with military posturing.

An aggressive response also risks stoking confrontation with the US, not yet six months after Xi and US President Joe Biden called to enhance communication during a face-to-face meeting in Bali.

“(A less overtly aggressive response) would imply that Beijing does not wish to escalate tension with the US to a level that can risk getting out of hand,” said Steve Tsang, director of the SOAS China Institute in London.

“A reset in US-China ties is not on the agenda, but an easing of tension is not beyond the realm of possibility.”

#68. Trump says US taking 'very seriously' any Iranian plot against Washington

Trump says US âlooking intoâ reported Iranian dirty bomb plot

President Donald Trump has said the US is taking "very seriously" the potential for any Iranian plot to attack Washington DC, after he was asked about a CNN report citing US intelligence assessments.

In the Philippines, where he attended the Asean Summit, Trump nodded when asked by a journalist about the report that Tehran was planning to detonate a so-called dirty bomb in the US capital and said: "We're looking into it."

CNN cited unnamed US intelligence officials suggesting Iran could target senior figures in Trump's administration with such an attack.

Iran called the claim "baseless." Concerns about dirty bombs grew after the 9/11 attacks in 2001, although no such attack has ever occurred.

Such a device would contain radioactive material - such as uranium - which would be scattered through the air when its conventional explosive detonated.

The BBC has not independently verified CNN's reporting.

During his comments to reporters in Manila, Trump was asked whether there was reason to believe an attack was imminent.

"I hope it's not," he said, "but we take it very seriously because they [the Iranians] happen to be crazy."

The US president was answering questions from reporters in the Philippines

In a statement before Trump's comments, White House spokesperson Lauren Bis wrote: "We remain vigilant monitoring any and all potential threats, but at this time there are no credible threats to the homeland."

Iranian foreign ministry spokesman Esmail Baghaei rebutted the CNN article and other US reports.

He posted on X: "Those who initiated the aggression against Iran are now seeking to lay the media groundwork for their sinister designs by manufacturing and disseminating baseless allegations."

Mohammad Bagher Ghalibaf, Iran's parliamentary speaker and chief negotiator in indirect talks with the US, was similarly dismissive of the CNN report.

"Same false flags, same war mongering, new logos," he posted on X.

Mick Mulroy, a former CIA paramilitary officer and deputy assistant defence secretary, told the BBC: "A dirty bomb is not difficult to make or deploy."

"Although it is not a weapon of mass destruction, it can be a weapon of mass disruption," he added.

Imran Bayoumi, a geostrategy expert with the Atlantic Council in Washington DC and former policy adviser to the defence department, said that it remains "unclear how serious" would be the threat of a supposed Iranian dirty bomb.

"But Iran has planned and threatened to attack the US in the past," he said. "It is also unclear why this report is becoming public now."

It comes as intelligence officials and members of the Trump administration seek congressional renewal of a key national security law.

Section 702 of the Foreign Intelligence Surveillance Act (Fisa), which allows the US to âspy on foreign nationals, expires next March.

#65. Nvidia partner Megaspeed being investigated by US government over chip exports

The United States has confirmed it is investigating computing company Megaspeed, suspecting it of violating US export controls by helping to ship tech giant Nvidia's chips to China.

In a joint statement, the US Department of Justice and Department of Commerce said they had been investigating Singapore-based firm Megaspeed since 2024.

It comes after questions were raised about the possibility high-end AI chips may have ended up in China despite US export restrictions.

Concerns emerged in recent weeks after Nvidia founder Jensen Huang revealed he had an extramarital affair, allegedly with former Megaspeed executive Alice Huang. US authorities suspect her of spying for Beijing, which China denies.

Jensen Huang resigned from his role as chief executive last week following revelations he had two affairs.

Megaspeed is the biggest buyer of Nvidia chips in South East Asia and is suspected of helping divert advanced chips to China. The Singapore-based company said on Sunday it had launched an internal investigation into its business.

The justice and commerce departments said on Monday that "it is in the public interest to provide the facts and address inaccurate information" regarding the investigation.

Laying out a timeline, it said it began investigating Megaspeed in 2024 "for potential export control violations".

In May 2025, Department of Commerce investigators requested records from Nvidia "concerning direct or indirect sales to Megaspeed". In June 2025, Nvidia placed Megaspeed on its 'no sell' list.

Agents from the Bureau of Industry and Security, in conjunction with Singaporean law enforcement officials, then conducted an on-site check at Megaspeed that month and interviewed Alice Huang, who was chief executive at the time.

Alice Huang and Jensen Huang pictured together in Taiwan in June 2024

Following this enforcement action, the Department of Justice took the lead role in the criminal investigation of Megaspeed.

The Nvidia scandal has created tensions between the US Treasury and Commerce departments over shipments of high-end chips to Megaspeed.

On Friday, Treasury Secretary Scott Bessent blamed the US Commerce Department, led by Howard Lutnick, for failing to properly oversee the export of the chips.

Lutnick has been called to testify before the Senate Banking Committee next month over his department's role in allowing Nvidia to export its advanced chips.

While Singapore does not prohibit the transfer of advanced AI chips to companies abroad, such moves can breach US export controls.

Huang has agreed to testify in Congress, including before the House Select Committee on China to discuss whether US national security was compromised by his affair.

The White House said on Monday it would further restrict China's access to AI chips from firms like Nvidia.

The reported affairs by Jensen Huang created a crisis for one of the world's most valuable firms - a major company in the AI industry and central to the US as it competes with China for dominance.

Trump downplays warnings of AI risks, citing rivalry with China

In the statement, the US government said it would "bring the full extent of the law against Megaspeed for any criminal conduct that may have occurred".

They added: "Sensitive technologies like semiconductors are among the most valuable and strategic assets of the US economy, and the Department of Justice and the Department of Commerce will remain vigilant in pursuing justice for those who want to compromise America's technological edge."

#63. Suspected Chinese spy balloon was able to transmit information back to Beijing

The Chinese spy balloon that transited the US earlier this year was able to capture imagery and collect some signals intelligence from US military sites, a source familiar with the matter tells CNN.

The balloon was able to transmit information back to Beijing in real time, the source said, and the US government still does not know for sure whether the Chinese government could wipe the balloon’s data as it received it. That raises questions about whether there is intelligence the balloon was able to gather that the US still doesn’t know about.

Still, the intelligence community has not been overly concerned about the information the balloon was able to gather, the person said, because it is not much more sophisticated than what Chinese satellites are able to glean as they orbit over similar locations.

A US intelligence official told CNN on Monday that “although analysis of the wreckage of the High Altitude Balloon remains ongoing, thus far, its flight over the United States does not appear to have provided critical new insights to the People’s Republic of China.”

The US also knew what the balloon’s path would be and was able to protect sensitive sites and censor some signals before the balloon was able to pick them up, officials have said.

As CNN has reported, the US intelligence community last year developed a method of tracking what it says is a fleet of these Chinese balloons operating across the globe, controlled by the Chinese military.

The FBI is still examining the balloon, but so far officials have been able to glean additional information about how the device worked, including the algorithms used for the balloon’s software and how it is powered and designed.

CNN has reached out to the National Security Council at the White House and the Pentagon for comment. NBC was first to report on the new intelligence.

The balloon first crossed into US airspace over Alaska in late January before passing through Canada and down into Montana, where it hovered for a few days, leading the US to believe it was trying to surveil sensitive military sites, like Malmstrom Air Force Base in Montana. It was eventually shot down by the US off the East Coast on February 4, and the incident further escalated tensions between Washington and Beijing, including the postponement of a diplomatic visit by US Secretary of State Antony Blinken to China.

A senior State Department official said in February that, as the balloon floated across the US, it “was capable of conducting signals intelligence collection operations.”

Gen. Glen VanHerck, the commander of US Northern Command and NORAD, said at the time that the US “did not assess” that the balloon “presented a significant collection hazard beyond what already exists in actionable technical means from the Chinese.”

The surveillance program, which includes a number of similar balloons, is in part run out of the small Chinese province of Hainan, officials told CNN. The US does not know the precise size of the fleet of Chinese surveillance balloons, but sources tell CNN that the program has conducted at least two dozen missions over at least five continents in recent years.

Roughly half a dozen of those flights have been within US airspace, although not necessarily over US territory, according to one official familiar with the intelligence.

China has maintained that the balloon was actually just a weather balloon thrown off course, and the US has been assessing the possibility that it was not deliberately maneuvered into the continental US by the Chinese government, CNN has reported.

Still, China did maintain some ability to maneuver the balloon, officials believe. And once the balloon was over Montana, China appeared to take advantage of its position to loiter over sensitive sites and try to collect intelligence.

This story has been updated with additional reporting.

CNN’s Alex Marquardt contributed to this report.

#61. Eurovision organisers criticise Malaysian removal of LGBT+ footage in first Asian contest

Broadcaster TV3 appears to cut footage of Thai drag performers and gay Philippine jury spokesperson

Eurovision’s organisers have said they are “surprised and disappointed” that the first Eurovision Asia was altered when it was broadcast in Malaysia, apparently removing LGBTQ+ imagery and people from the show.

The European Broadcasting Union (EBU), the alliance of public broadcasters that owns Eurovision, said it would write to Voxovation, the company producing the Asian contest under licence, and ask it to establish with the Malaysian broadcaster TV3 why it happened.

Footage circulating online of the contest, which was won by the Philippines’ Carmelle, showed the first of two cuts during the voting in Saturday’s final in Bangkok, when the Philippines’ spokesperson, Vice Ganda, awarded the Philippine jury’s points.

Vice Ganda is gay and identifies as non-binary. The comedian is among the biggest stars on Philippine television, has fronted the country’s most watched daytime show, and has campaigned for LGBTQ+ acceptance at home.

Announcing a national jury’s points is one of the most visible roles in a Eurovision final. Each country’s spokesperson appears in turn to reveal how their jury has ranked the other entries.

While the international feed showed Vice Ganda announcing that the Philippine jury had given its 12 points, the maximum, to the Malaysian entrant, Aina Abdul, TV3 viewers saw only the scoreboard.

Separate footage shows part of the show’s opening number showcasing Thai culture, in which drag performers appeared on street food carts, replaced with a wide shot taken from rehearsals.

Eurovision fans accused the channel of “censorship”. The Guardian has not been able to review TV3’s broadcast independently as its livestream is no longer available online.

Martin Green, the director of the Eurovision song contest, said: “We are surprised and disappointed to learn that the broadcast of Eurovision song contest Asia was altered in Malaysia.

“We take this matter seriously and will be writing to Voxovation, the producers of the show, to seek clarification and ask them to establish with Malaysian broadcaster TV3 why this happened.

“This appears to be isolated to the Malaysian broadcast of the show, and all other transmissions were not altered.”

Voxovation said in a statement that it was aware of the concerns raised and was “looking into the matter with the broadcaster to establish the circumstances … We are working with the Malaysian broadcaster to make sure this doesn’t happen again.”

Neither the EBU nor Voxovation said whether the contest’s participation agreement required broadcasters to carry the show unedited or what the consequences would be if one did not.

The Guardian has asked Media Prima, Malaysia’s largest media group and TV3’s owner, for comment.

This is not the first time the wider Eurovision song contest has faced allegations of censorship. In May 2018 the EBU terminated its partnership with the Chinese streaming service Mango TV after it dropped the Irish and Albanian entries and blurred a rainbow flag in the audience, saying such censorship was not in line with its “values of universality and inclusivity”.

Asked before Saturday’s final how the EBU’s commitment to its values squared with the laws in Malaysia and Bangladesh, the other competing country where same-sex relations are criminalised, the EBU’s head of communications, Dave Goodman, said the contest’s values were “non-negotiable”.

Malaysia has some of the harshest anti-LGBTQ+ laws in Asia, with oral and anal sex being punishable by up to 20 years in prison.

Rights groups have warned of a hardening climate. In November last year, authorities raided a men-only spa in Kuala Lumpur and detained more than 200 men, though no charges followed. And in 2022, police raided an LGBTQ+ Halloween party in the capital and arrested 20 people.

In 2023, a festival appearance by the British band the 1975, whose singer criticised the country’s homophobic laws and kissed a male bandmate on stage, led to the event being cancelled and prompted tighter entry rules for foreign performers.

TV3 has faced pressure over LGBTQ+ visibility before. In February 2025, it apologised after male guests arrived at a red carpet event in clothing resembling women’s dress.

#59. Indian woman gang-raped while pregnant in 2002 'heartbroken' after two perpetrators bailed

The attack on Bilkis Bano and her family was one of the most horrific crimes during the Gujarat riots

The article has details that some readers may find disturbing

A pregnant Muslim woman who was gang-raped and saw 14 family members murdered during the 2002 Gujarat riots has spoken of her "pain, shock and disbelief" after two of her rapists were recently granted bail.

Bilkis Bano said in a statement that she heard about the top court order through press reports and vowed to fight back.

The freed men are from a group of 11 convicts - part of a Hindu mob - serving life terms for the attack on Bano.

All the men were also freed in 2022 after a Gujarat government order. That decision, and the celebrations as they left prison, caused global outrage. In 2024, the Supreme Court overturned the order.

In a short three-page order dated 28 October, the Supreme Court granted bail to Bipinchand Kanaiyalal Joshi and Pradip Ramanlal Modhiya and suspended their sentences while their petition challenging a 2017 Bombay High Court order is being heard.

The judges explained that the early release was because the two convicts "have been under incarceration for a period of more than 17 years" and "that one of the appellants has a severe handicap".

On Tuesday, Bano's lawyer Shobha Gupta told the BBC that the news of Joshi and Modhiya's release had left "Bilkis heartbroken".

"She has to restart her battle for justice. She had no information that this case was coming up in the Supreme Court. What is troubling for us is that every time something like this happens, we worry that it could affect her faith in judiciary."

The riots began after a fire on a passenger train in Godhra town killed 60 Hindu pilgrims

Gupta said it was difficult to understand why the convicts were being released because "it's not a routine criminal case, it's a case that shocks the conscience of the world, of entire humanity".

The attack on Bano and her family was one of the most horrific crimes during the Gujarat riots, which began after 60 Hindu pilgrims were killed in a fire on a passenger train in the town of Godhra.

Hindu mobs attacked Muslim neighbourhoods, blaming them for starting the fire. The riots continued for three days and more than 1,000 people died, most of them Muslims.

When rioters attacked Bano's village and started setting Muslim homes on fire, she and 16 relatives went on the run. She was 19, had a three-year-old daughter and was pregnant with her second child.

They were attacked on the morning of 3 March 2002 by a group of men "with swords and sticks", she told me in 2017. Her three-year-old daughter, she said, was snatched from her lap and killed.

How a Gujarat riot victim stood up to her rapists

India Supreme Court cancels release of 2002 riots rapists

India PM Modi's government okayed rapists' release

Her attackers were her neighbours, men she had seen almost daily while growing up. They tore off her clothes and several of them raped her, ignoring her pleas for mercy.

Her cousin, who had given birth two days earlier, was raped and murdered and her newborn baby was killed too.

Bano survived only because she lost consciousness and her attackers left, believing she was dead. Two boys - seven and four - were the only other survivors of the massacre.

The riots continued for three days and more than 1,000 people died, most of them Muslims

Her fight for justice has been long and nightmarish. The first arrests were made only in 2004 after the Supreme Court called in federal investigators and transferred her case to Mumbai, saying that courts in Gujarat could not deliver justice to her.

In her statement released on Monday night, Bano spoke of "fresh tears of pain, shock and disbelief".

"I still cannot believe that once again, for the second time, an order has been passed by the Supreme Court without calling me up and without hearing me," she said.

"For 24 long years, I have stood up to fight for justice... again and again. Yet today, I find myself losing the very justice I had fought so hard to win.... And I am scared of losing faith in justice, yet again."

Bano vowed to "fight this order... for my children, who do not deserve to live haunted by fears and scars for the rest of their lives".

"I do this for my three-year-old Saleha who deserved to live and whose memory demands justice. I do this for women everywhere. Because no woman, no survivor, no citizen should have to endure this eternal test of belonging and equality, and fight for justice for the rest of her life."

Prime Minister Narendra Modi was Gujarat chief minister at the time of the riots, and was criticised for not doing enough to prevent the carnage. He has always denied wrongdoing and has not apologised for the riots.

In 2013, a Supreme Court panel also said that there was insufficient evidence to prosecute him. But critics have continued to blame him for the riots happening on his watch.

#57. Rio Tinto agrees to pay US$250m to begin repairing ‘devastation’ left by Panguna mine

Community leader says fund gives chance to find solutions to ‘huge problems we have been living with since the days of the mine’s operation’

Anglo-Australian miner Rio Tinto has agreed to pay a quarter-of-a-billion dollars to begin the clean-up of the toxic Panguna mine site it abandoned more than three decades ago.

Panguna, once one of the world’s most profitable gold and copper mines, was shuttered in 1989 after violent protests over environmental damage and the inequitable division of the mine’s profits sparked a decade-long civil war on the Papua New Guinean island of Bougainville.

But the sprawling mine pit, once a mountain, remains a scar in the middle of the island, with vast pillars of rubble at risk of collapse, and polluted rivers running bright blue with toxins.

Rio Tinto was forced from the island and has never returned, but in recent years has been part of a detailed consultation process with landowners and government. It has now agreed to voluntarily contribute US$250m (A$370m) towards the remediation of the mine site.

Theonila Roka Matbob, a former cabinet minister in Bougainville’s autonomous government and Panguna community leader, said the fund was a vital contribution towards repairing the “devastation left by the Panguna mine”.

“We have a lot of work still ahead of us, but the establishment of this fund gives us the chance to find solutions to the huge problems we have been living with since the days of the mine’s operation.

“No amount of money will restore the damage caused to the environment in Panguna, but this sets a foundation for us to invest in addressing the environmental issues and invest in our children’s futures as well.”

Roka Matbob said the most fundamental remediation was to improve “accessibility” for the people living around Panguna – “accessibility to clean drinking water that is safe and secure … and infrastructure, in bridges that makes people’s access to trade, to school, to health[care], much easier. This is one critical area that immediately needs attention.”

The president of Bougainville, Ishmael Toroama, seeking to become the world’s newest head of state at the forefront of a long-running independence movement on the island, said the money would make a “meaningful difference” to villages and communities that had suffered from the impacts of the mine’s pollution.

“This work will deliver benefits not only for people today, but for future generations. Continued engagement with our communities will be essential to ensuring the work responds to local priorities and delivers lasting outcomes.”

The Rio commitment has emerged from the Panguna Mine Legacy Impact Assessment consultation process, which was initiated in response to a human rights complaint brought by the Australian-based Human Rights Law Centre, with Roka Matbob as the lead complainant.

Keren Adams, the HRLC’s legal director, said establishing the fund had taken years of work.

“It is a testament to the courage and persistence of community leaders, and the willingness of all parties to engage in difficult but constructive dialogue,” she said.

“The significant funding contributed by Rio Tinto creates a real opportunity to now address an environmental disaster that has remained unresolved for decades.”

Mark Davies, Rio Tinto’s chief safety and technical officer, said the establishment of the fund was an “important milestone … intended to support the transition from the assessment to practical remedy implementation”.

“Community engagement is an important next step to identify priorities and we remain committed to working with communities to shape the activities supported by the fund.”

The quarter-of-a-billion dollars will not be sufficient to entirely remediate the mine site, but it is being seen as a major step towards rehabilitation.

The funding commitment for Panguna comes at an acutely sensitive time for the restive Bougainville island.

The mine has become inextricably linked with the Bougainville’s political future: restarting operations at Panguna – believed to still hold at least $60bn in unrealised reserves – is seen by many, including the president, as the fundamental financial basis for the island’s independent future. Indian miner Lloyds has been granted a 25-year large-scale mining lease, but any future mining is years from starting.

Bougainville, a group of islands off Papua New Guinea to the north of Australia, has been variously controlled by Britain, Germany, Japan, Australia and PNG.

It declared independence in 1975 as the Republic of North Solomons, but was absorbed into the newly independent PNG weeks later.

Its close ethnic ties and proximity to the Solomons archipelago means it has always sat uneasily with Port Moresby’s rule.

That discontent came to a violent head in 1988 after a long-running dispute over the lucrative Panguna copper and gold mine.

The mine, carved from a mountain in central Bougainville, was critically important to PNG, at one point generating more than 45% of the country's national export revenue.

Multinational Rio Tinto and other international investors earned nearly US$2bn from the mine over 17 years of operation. But less than 1% of the mine’s profits went to Bougainvilleans, whose homes and lands had been destroyed by it.

Under the leadership of a former mine worker, Francis Ona, disaffected Bougainvilleans ran a sabotage campaign which forced the mine’s closure in 1989.

The PNG Government responded by sending in police, then soldiers, to restore law and order. Bougainville was blockaded and the island descended into a brutal civil war that ran for a decade, and saw as many as 20,000 people die.

PNG later hired international mercenaries to end the conflict (the notorious Sandline affair), before a peace agreement was signed in 2001.

In a 2019 referendum, 97.7% of Bougainvilleans voted in favour of independence. Many argue that reopening Panguna mine will be key to the island’s economic future as an independent nation.

Rio Tinto and other international investors earned nearly $2bn from Panguna over 17 years of operation from 1972. But less than 1% of the mine’s profits went to Bougainvilleans, whose homes and lands were destroyed by it.

Disaffected landowners ran a sabotage campaign that forced Panguna’s closure in 1989. The PNG government responded by sending in police, then soldiers, to restore law and order. Bougainville was blockaded and the island descended into a brutal civil war – known as “the crisis” – that ran for a decade. Between 15,000 and 20,000 people were killed, roughly 10% of the island’s population.

A recent environmental impact assessment found the abandoned mine remains an acute threat to people’s lives, including from landslides, levee collapses and floods. Untouched, the river delta will stay polluted for a century.

The future of Bougainville’s independence campaign is now before the PNG parliament, which will decide whether or not to recognise a UN-backed independence referendum held on the island in 2019, in which 97.7% of voters were in favour of Bougainville seceding and becoming an independent country.

The PNG parliament, which heard lengthy and impassioned debate over Bougainville last month, appears almost certain to vote against recognising the referendum result.

But Toroama, who has staked his presidency on achieving political independence, has said, regardless of the parliament’s decision, Bougainville will enter a period of “self-government” from September next year, before seceding, breaking away from PNG as a fully fledged independent nation, by 2030.

#54. Hundreds arrested in anti-Trump protests in Philippines amid anger over Iran war and AI pact

Organisers say up to 10,000 people could join next round of demonstrations as US president arrives for Asean summit

Hundreds of people have been arrested in heated demonstrations against Donald Trump in Manila, as the US president arrived in the city for a leaders’ summit.

Organisers claimed as many as 10,000 people would be joining protests against Trump in the capital of the Philippines on Tuesday, where he is attending the Association of Southeast Asian Nations (Asean) summit. The crowds were expected to try to march to the summit site.

Authorities have arrested nearly 300 people in widespread protests held in the days leading up to the summit, with large parts of the capital closed off and tight security in place.

Some burned effigies of Trump and faced off with police officers armed with batons and shields. Slogans on placards called the American leader the world’s “No 1 terrorist” in protests held on Monday.

Demonstrators angry at the cost of living and energy shock caused by the Iran war, and the new US-led Pax Silica initiative on AI and critical minerals, tried to reach the summit at the Philippine International Convention Center on Monday, but were blocked by police.

Pax Silica has sparked tensions in the Philippines over plans to build a tech hub outside Manila that critics say will affect water and energy supplies, and displace members of the Ayta Indigenous community.

Large sections of central Manila were closed to the public as part of the security cordon and public holidays put in place for the Asean summit.

After departing from Fairbanks in Alaska, Trump emerged from Air Force One at Ninoy Aquino International airport at 5.54pm local time (0954 GMT), live video showed.

Trump will meet with the leaders of Australia, Japan and India on Tuesday night before heading to Shenzhen in China on Wednesday. There he will attend the Asia Pacific Economic Cooperation (Apec) summit and hold trilateral talks with China’s president, Xi Jinping, and Vladimir Putin of Russia.

The backlash against Trump follows the ousting of the boss of Nvidia, Jensen Huang, and allegations members of the US administration, including the commerce secretary, Howard Lutnick, knew about his affair with an alleged Chinese spy.

Trump’s delegation was expected to include Elon Musk.

Protest leader Renato Reyes blamed the arrests in Manila on the heavy handed response of the government on the president, Ferdinand Marcos Jr, calling Monday the largest single day of police arrests since Marcos came to office in 2022.

“It is really crazy that the Philippine government is rolling out the red carpet for someone who has been so flatly rejected by the American people,” Reyes said. “Trump started a war that devastated the global economy and pushed oil prices in the Philippines to historic highs. It has caused untold suffering on the Filipino people.

“So, yes, Trump will have to pay for his crimes and he will have to be answerable, not just to the American people, but to the people of the world.”

The Philippine national police said some officers were injured during confrontations with demonstrators opposed to the summit, including when objects were allegedly thrown at officers.

Pax Silica will include a technology hub planned at New Clark City in Tarlac, about 100km from Manila.

Intended to bolster western stocks of AI technology and reduce “excessive dependencies” on single source supply chains, including from China, Pax Silica will provide chips and critical minerals to 26 participating signatory governments.

Amnesty International said demonstrations should not be forcibly or violently dispersed.

“Reports of arrests during the protests demand immediate scrutiny,” Amnesty’s director for the Philippines, Ritz Lee Santos III, said. “Authorities must explain the specific grounds for each arrest and any continued detention. Anyone detained solely for peacefully exercising their rights must be released immediately.”

#52. Trump in Asia ahead of talks with Russian and Chinese presidents

US President Donald Trump has arrived in the Philippines' capital on the first leg of a trip to Asia, which will end in a meeting in China with its President Xi Jinping and his Russian counterpart Vladimir Putin.

A red carpet was rolled out for Trump as he walked off Air Force One in Manila. A band played one of his favourite songs, Y.M.C.A., before he was whisked away by helicopter to a hotel.

Trump's first meeting at the Asean Summit was with US partners in the Quad - India's Narendra Modi, whom he described as a "very tough man", Japan's Sanae Takaichi, who "may be even tougher", and Australia's Anthony Albanese.

"Our countries are the four corners of the free, open, stable, prosperous Indo-Pacific," the US president said.

"We have outstanding relationships and co-ordinate closely... my phone is always open," Trump added.

Many countries in South East Asia have been hit hard by Trump's tariffs and his war on Iran - but their exports to the US rose 30% last year thanks to the AI boom for which the region manufactures many of the components.

The US is hoping to draw more countries here into its "Pax Silica" tech and AI ecosystem, and away from China.

But China's huge influence in this region cannot easily be challenged, and certainly not in the few hours Trump is in the Philippines.

Later on Tuesday, Trump signed a trade deal with Thailand alongside the country's Prime Minister Anutin Charnvirakul.

The US is Thailand's largest export market, accounting for 24% of its total shipments this year.

Trump then joined South East Asia leaders at a dinner, telling those gathered: "This is one of the world's most vibrant and productive regions filled with the tremendous potential."

After an overnight stay in Manila, the US president will fly to Shenzhen in China to attend a summit of Asia Pacific leaders and have nuclear talks with Xi Jinping and Vladimir Putin.

Asked if he would also be open to a one-on-one meeting with Putin, Trump said: "If it's available, I would".

#48. William and Kate celebrate Earthshot Prize winners in Mumbai

Watch: William and Catherine walk green carpet in Mumbai for Earthshot Prize

The Prince and Princess of Wales have said environmental innovators are changing the lives of future generations as they celebrated this year's winners of the Earthshot Prize.

Five award-winners were named at a ceremony in Mumbai, India, and will each receive Â£1m to scale up their projects.

One of the winners was a country - with Bhutan being recognised for its environmental policies that help to safeguard water supplies for nearly a fifth of the world's population.

Indian cricket legend Sachin Tendulkar, actor Rami Malek and Bollywood stars Alia Bhatt and Sonam Kapoor were among those who attended the ceremony, which saw performances from Take That and Bridgerton's Simone Ashley.

The annual event, founded by Prince William and now in its sixth year, was hosted by Karan Johar, presenter of The Traitors India and one of the country's most well-known film makers.

Prince William was joined by Catherine at the ceremony as the royal couple continued their first official overseas trip together in four years. Catherine's cancer treatment has limited the amount she has travelled internationally.

They arrived on the event's green carpet wearing outfits celebrating Indian fashion.

Prince William wore a bespoke Bandhgala, a classic Indian formal jacket, while Catherine was dressed in an Anarkali dress by Mahima Mahajan made from handwoven silk Georgette with traditional Banarasi gold detailing.

During his speech, Prince William said this decade was the moment when positive change becomes irreversible" and remarked that history was "not written by challenges alone", but also by those who rose to meet them.

He added that "around the world, we are witnessing human ingenuity on a remarkable scale".

Separately, Catherine highlighted the "hope" represented by entrepreneurs, organisations and scientists tackling the environmental crisis.

The princess, who presented an award, also said: "Just as the natural world finds ways to adapt and flourish, so too can we, when we nurture new ideas and give them the opportunity to grow.

"The people we're celebrating tonight are a huge part of that story. Their work will inspire and create seismic change that will improve lives for generations to come."

William and Catherine talk with guests during the awards ceremony in Mumbai

The royal couple also spoke to those performing, which included Take That

There are five Earthshots, or goals, for which an award is given: Protect and Restore Nature; Clean Our Air; Revive Our Oceans; Build a Waste-Free World; and Fix Our Climate.

Five winners were chosen from a list of 15 finalists who attended Tuesday's awards ceremony.

Protect and Restore Nature: Bhutan, the country that helps to protect the water security of 1.6 billion people across South Asia and has a national nature coalition that aims to revive forests and rivers by restoring 50,000 hectares of damaged land

Build a Waste-Free World: PFAS-Free France, a national campaign that helped secure landmark legislation banning 'forever chemicals' in France by 2030, and hopes to extend the ban to more major countries

Revive Our Oceans: Niue Ocean Wide Trust, the Pacific island's scheme that has safeguarded marine life by investing in conservation to fund the long-term protection of its vast waters

Clean Our Air: Mahindra Last Mile Mobility, a scheme rolling out up to one million electric rickshaws across India with the aim to drasitically cut one of the world's largest sources of on-road air pollution

Fix Our Climate: Ãrsted, a global energy company that has cut its direct carbon emissions by more than 98% by transforming from being highly coal-intensive to a leader in offshore wind

Indian actor Sonam Kapoor was among the famous guests invited to attend the annual awards night

Indian cricket legend Sachin Tendulkar (right) also appeared alongside his wife Anjali (second right), daughter Sara (second left) and daughter-in-law Saaniya (left)

The Earthshot prize, first held in 2020, was inspired by former US President John F Kennedy's Moonshot project, which challenged scientists to get astronauts to the Moon and back safely.

Tuesday's event also saw appearances on stage from Earthshot ambassadors Robert Irwin, the Australian conservationist, and Nomzamo Mbatha, the South African actor.

As well as Take That, there were also musical performances from a number of Indian artists including Shreya Ghoshal, composer and pianist Merlin D'Souza and all-female hip hop collective Wild Wild Women.

The dazzling awards night was brought to a close by musician and composer Shashwat Sachdev.

After driving an electric rickshaw, William said he would've liked a longer promenade so he could "see what the top speed was"

Earlier on Tuesday, William and Catherine drove in an electric rickshaw to meet the 15 finalists.

As William stepped from the vehicle, which is manufactured by prize winner Mahindra Last Mile Mobility and has a top speed of 55mph, he said: "It was quite good fun, really good fun."

The couple arrived in India on Monday for the three-day visit, which is expected to also include a meeting with India's Prime Minister Narendra Modi on Wednesday.

Their first engagement saw them play a game of street - or "gully" cricket - in Mumbai alongside Tendulkar.

#47. Hi-tech Shenzhen takes centre stage as Xi sends ‘Chinamaxxing’ message at Apec summit

Host city offers showcase of China’s rise into a confident and innovative power central to global economy

In a Shenzhen square on a balmy evening, locals and tourists watched the dozens of glinting towers that make up the city’s skyline ripple through the colour spectrum – cyan and violet, cyber green, China’s national scarlet and gold – repeatedly flashing the words “Apec 2026”.

The Asia-Pacific Economic Cooperation summit is a dream showcase for one of China’s most advanced cities, and the country’s leader, Xi Jinping, is basking in its neon glow.

On the sidelines, a series of high-profile bilateral meetings and the announcement of a historic three-way summit with Donald Trump and Vladimir Putin have cemented Xi’s status as an agenda setter on the global stage.

The broader Apec summit, meanwhile, provides an opportunity for China to present itself as a steward of multilateralism against what it terms “unilateralism and protectionism” – that is to say, trade measures taken against Beijing by countries such as the US.

All this is set against the futuristic backdrop that is Shenzhen, a city that perhaps better than no other embodies China’s rise into a confident, technologically innovative power central to the global economy.

A photo display in the Apec media centre charts Shenzhen’s transformation from sparsely populated marshland, calling it “a miracle in the history of world development created by the Chinese people”.

“[Xi] wants people to go home Chinamaxxing,” said Prof Astrid Nordin, of King’s College London. “Shenzhen is itself part of the message. It’s a showcase of Chinese technological and economic achievements, and of China’s claim that it offers opportunities for prosperity in a world increasingly characterised by uncertainty and conflict.”

Like the light show, the summit is being carefully coordinated. The Financial Times cited people familiar with the matter as saying it would be one of the most tightly controlled in the organisation’s history, with leaders having to travel to a state guesthouse beside Shenzhen’s tiger safari park to meet Xi. The paper said diplomats had grumbled that the format “seems designed to reduce the free-flowing discussion characteristic of prior editions”.

Setting out its expectations for the summit, China’s foreign ministry has said it looks forward to “practical results in key areas such as trade, connectivity, digital economy, AI and so on”.

Already, Xi has announced trade wins with Canada’s Mark Carney, extending remission measures for certain Chinese steel and aluminium products by two years in exchange for tariff relief on Canadian pork, fish and seafood exports.

A meeting with Papua New ⁠Guinea’s leader, meanwhile, showed how Beijing’s geopolitical priorities have been absorbed and reflected by its allies, with James Marape recounting to Xi how he had closed Taiwan’s office in his country despite domestic challenges in doing so.

The most important meeting will be between Xi and Trump. The leaders of the world’s two superpowers are expected to tackle a broad range of issues including AI and trade. In a move that could strain the fragile tariff truce that the two leaders first agreed last year, the Trump administration said on Monday it would further restrict China’s access to advanced AI chips.

Christopher Adams, a senior adviser at the law firm Covington who was at the centre of trade negotiations with China for the Obama and first Trump administrations, said: “The trade truce may increasingly look like a ceasefire in a war where major combat operations remain on hold but skirmishes are escalating to the point where the line between peace and war is blurred.

“I don’t see pressure to announce a further extension of the trade truce in Shenzhen. If a significant extension is announced, that would be an upside surprise.”

China has not hesitated to leverage Apec’s multinational platform in service of its arguments. A commentary by the state news agency Xinhua on Tuesday said: “Amid deepening geopolitical rivalries and growing global divisions, [China’s hosting] sends a clear signal that great-power competition need not be a zero-sum game, and that one country’s development need not come at the expense of another.”

The chair of the Apec senior officials’ meeting, Chen Xu, said Beijing had made trade “openness” a key focus of its Apec year and looked forward to “achieving more practical outcomes on the trade front”.

The much anticipated trilateral summit with Trump and Putin, on the other hand, could prove largely symbolic. For Xi, “it provides the appearance of China shaping the world order as an equal to the United States,” said Dr Kevork Oskanian, an expert on Russia at the University of Exeter. “Agreement on substantial issues, which would, in any case, require extensive preparatory negotiations, is unlikely.”

For China’s ruling Communist party, the political pomp has another key purpose. Nordin said summits were “occasions for leaders to tell stories about the world, about their country’s place in it … and one of the most important audiences for those stories is at home.”

For the locals watching the skyscraper light show on Monday night, the symbolism was not lost. China’s export might had helped it become increasingly powerful on the world stage, said one local man, who did not give his name. “It’s so great that the summit will take place in Shenzhen,” he said. “I feel very proud.”

#43. American soldier arrested by UK police on suspicion of spying taken into US military custody

Lt Col Brandon Carroll Tyner was arrested on 10 November

A US Army officer arrested in the UK on suspicion of spying for Russia earlier this month has been taken into US military custody, British counter-terror police have said.

The serviceman has been identified as Lt Col Brandon Carroll Tyner, BBC News understands. The 41-year-old - who served in the US Army since 2008 - was arrested near Gloucester last week by British counter-terror police.

Tyner was based at Imjin Barracks in Innsworth, Gloucestershire, the headquarters of Nato's Allied Rapid Reaction Corps.

Counter Terrorism Policing said he is now in US custody over suspected national security and espionage allegations. At the time of his arrest, Russia said it had "nothing to do with it".

British police previously said he was being held in London after he was arrested under section one and section three of the National Security Act 2023, which relate to obtaining or disclosing protected information, and assisting a foreign intelligence service.

The day after Tyner's arrest on 10 November, Kremlin spokesman Dmitry Peskov said he was "unaware" of what it was connected to.

"We have nothing to do with it, and it has nothing to do with us," he told reporters in Moscow.

The US serviceman is understood to have been based at Imjin Barracks in Gloucestershire

On Tuesday, Assistant Commissioner Laurence Taylor, head of Counter Terrorism Policing, said: "Subsequently, a decision was made to pass the investigation to them [their American partners] to progress."

"These are extremely serious allegations, and our collective response should send a strong message that we will act swiftly and robustly where we suspect this kind of espionage activity taking place," he added.

Tyner's arrest was the result of a long-running operation by MI5 - the UK's domestic intelligence and security agency - in collaboration with the FBI, the BBC understands.

He had previously served in Afghanistan and Iraq, but at the time of his arrest was working with NATO's Allied Rapid Reaction Corps near Gloucester - where he was said to be entrusted with sensitive information which would have been extremely damaging if leaked to Russia.

Detectives sent the Crown Prosecution Service a file on the senior US soldier's alleged spying for consideration - allegations described by the head of Counter Terrorism Policing as "extremely serious."

But at that stage the Attorney General's Office in London got involved. As did the US authorities.

And now the decision has been taken that Lieutenant Colonel Tyner should be taken into custody by the US Military.

It means he will not stand trial in the UK, but under US Uniform Code of Military Justice.

FBI director Kash Patel said an army officer was in custody "as the result of an espionage investigation and the close cooperation" between the bureau, US Army and various UK partners.

"Together we worked to identify a potential threat to national security and take swift action," he said.

"This FBI has zero tolerance for anyone who betrays their oath to the United States or puts our national security at risk."

US Brig Gen Sean Stinchon said the case should serve as a "stark warning to any service member suspected of violating the oath" they all take.

"Whenever we suspect somebody of betraying the trust of their fellow soldiers and jeopardising our collective defence, we will identify you, and we will ensure justice is served," he added.

#41. Jensen Huang to testify before Congress on ‘potential breach of national security’

High-stakes hearing comes in wake of resignation from Nvidia after alleged affair with suspected Chinese spy

#38. US loses measles elimination status after 26 years amid record case numbers

BBC's Madeline Halpert explains why the US lost its measles elimination status and what it could mean.

After more than two decades, the US has lost its measles-free status, following two years of record case numbers and deaths.

The Pan American Health Organization (PAHO), a specialised service of the United Nations, determined the US has had uninterrupted transmission of the same strain of measles for more than 12 months.

Dr Jarbas Barbosa, director of PAHO, announced that Mexico and Bolivia have also lost their measles elimination status, attributing the resurgence in the region to persistent "gaps in vaccination".

The US Centers for Disease Control and Prevention (CDC) said it was committed to "regaining measles elimination status" by expanding access to vaccines.

The US has seen 5,400 cases of measles this year alone, with 10 measles-related deaths, the most the country has seen since the 1990s.

The measles virus - with its signature blotchy red rash - used to infect millions of children a year before a vaccine was invented. The extremely contagious virus can be fatal, and some patients can experience long-term complications including encephalitis and immune amnesia, which resets the immune system, leaving it with only limited ability to respond to new infections.

The US recommends two measles, mumps and rubella (MMR) shots for children between ages one and six, which is 97% effective in protecting against the illness.

PAHO, a group within the World Health Organization, revoked the US's measles-elimination after determining that the virus had circulated nonstop for more than a year.

"These figures are a serious warning for our region," PAHO's director Barbosa said at a news conference on Tuesday. "Measles is resurging globally amid persistent gaps in vaccination."

He added that Canada, which lost its status of having eliminated measles last year after seeing several large outbreaks, has made strides this year to interrupt transmission.

Daniel Salas, the executive manager of immunisations at PAHO, said that the US and others who lost elimination status would be required to submit corrective action plans outlining how they would attempt to improve vaccination rates.

US Health Secretary Robert F Kennedy Jr Kennedy has attempted to make a series of sweeping changes to US vaccine recommendations and policies. The vaccine sceptic has also frequently cast doubt on the safety of MMR shot, as vaccination rates have declined across the US in recent years.

Kennedy has argued that the rise in measles cases started before he took the helm at HHS and that neighbouring countries have also seen more infections.

Responding to Tuesday's announcement, the CDC said the agency and its partners were "committed to stopping measles outbreaks, protecting Americans, and regaining measles elimination status".

"Together, we will encourage MMR vaccination, expand access to vaccines, and help communities close gaps in protection," the agency said.

CDC Director Erica Schwartz said the measles elimination designation was not what protected Americans.

"Vaccination does," she said. "We know where transmission is occurring and we've invested $100 million in state and local health departments to reach those communities."

In a post on social media, Kennedy said: "I continue to recommend MMR vaccination as the most effective way to prevent measles."

"We are working to close vaccination gaps and rebuild public trust," he also said. "We will answer families' questions honestly about vaccine benefits and risks and work with trusted health care providers and community leaders to prevent measles, limit its spread, and support timely treatment."

The UK, Spain and Austria were among European nations that lost their measles-free status earlier this year, along with countries including Russia, Azerbaijan and Uzbekistan.

Nuwan Gunawardhana, an assistant professor of medicine with Columbia University Irving Medical Center, said the US losing its elimination status was "a huge leap backwards".

The US was the first country to eliminate measles in the Americas thanks to vaccination campaigns, noted William Moss, a paediatric infectious disease specialist at the Johns Hopkins Bloomberg School of Public Health.

Dr Gunawardhana said it took massive efforts from public health officials and clinicians to build trust with patients to increase uptake of the MMR vaccine, leading to decades in the US with few infections and deaths.

But that trust has been steadily eroding since the Covid-19 pandemic, when Dr Gunawardhana said patients were confronted with misinformation about the safety of vaccines and lost even more trust in public health systems.

The vaccination rate for MMR across the US has fallen to 92.4%, below the 95% level that provides herd immunity, which limits the spread of measles and protects those who cannot get vaccinated, including newborns.

The vaccination rate for MMR across the US has fallen to 92.4%, below the 95% level that provides herd immunity - which limits the spread of measles

An outbreak in Pennsylvania has infected over 1,400 people - likely a large underestimate - and killed seven people, according to state officials.

Kennedy, the health secretary, has sparred with Pennsylvania officials over how it is counting the measles deaths, with CDC declining for now to record some of the deaths in its database.

Last year, the health secretary fired all the members of a top vaccine advisory panel and replaced them with his own appointees, some of whom were vaccine critics.

The panel recommended a number of vaccine changes - including no longer requiring the Hepatitis B vaccine for all newborns - but those recommendations were blocked by a federal judge.

President Donald Trump this year also cast doubt on the safety of the MMR vaccine, issuing an executive order for the shot to be split into three separate immunisations for measles, mumps and rubella, a move public health experts say is costly and could sow confusion about an already safe vaccine.

Doctors and public health officials say the misinformation from Kennedy and Trump has played a major role in falling vaccination rates.

"It's confusing patients. It's confusing families," Dr Gunawardhana said. "We need a clear message from the top."

The federal government's vaccine manoeuvres have left public health officials and states to fill in the gaps, offering their own separate recommendations upholding decades-long practices.
