#16. Trump’s Whirlwind Asia Trip Begins in the Philippines

President Trump’s 48-hour trip started with a meeting with Southeast Asian leaders in Manila. He will later meet Xi Jinping of China and Vladimir Putin of Russia in Shenzhen, a Chinese economic hub.

President Trump landed in Manila on Tuesday to begin a 48-hour Asia trip in which he will meet with Xi Jinping of China and other world leaders, with an unpredictable agenda that could include the future of American tariffs, nuclear arms control, the war in Ukraine and U.S. restrictions on advanced A.I. chips.

Mr. Trump was scheduled to meet with President Ferdinand R. Marcos Jr. of the Philippines and attend a gathering of Southeast Asian leaders. After less than 24 hours in Manila, he will head to the APEC economic summit in Shenzhen, China, where he was expected to meet with Mr. Xi and President Vladimir V. Putin of Russia.

The brief itinerary appears to reflect Mr. Trump’s preferences. He dislikes big summits where he often is not the sole subject of attention. And he enjoys meeting with leaders of powerful nations — especially Mr. Xi and Mr. Putin, two authoritarian leaders he often talks about in admiring terms.

Mr. Trump will be meeting Mr. Xi for the third time this year, and a three-way meeting between China, Russia and the United States would be the first of its kind.

On Tuesday, Mr. Trump caught the end of a dayslong meeting of the Association of Southeast Asian Nations, or ASEAN, after issuing tariffs on countries across the region, even allies. Those moves added to a sentiment in Asia that the United States was not a reliable partner but rather one that treasured its commercial interests over its security interests. The only event of substance on the president’s schedule on Tuesday — signing what he had called a “trade deal” with Thailand — did not happen because Mr. Trump said it was still being finalized.

Mr. Trump met with leaders from the so-called Quad — the United States, India, Japan and Australia — soon after his arrival. All three partners are smarting from high energy prices triggered by the Iran war, a conflict Mr. Trump never discussed with them before launching it in late February.

In Shenzhen — the heart of China’s robotics and artificial intelligence industries — Mr. Trump will pursue détente with Mr. Xi. He needs to extend a soon-expiring trade truce that limited tariffs on Chinese goods entering the United States in return for a Chinese commitment not to cut off rare earths and magnets.

Then comes the main event: the meeting with Mr. Xi and Mr. Putin. Referring to the trilateral meeting, Mr. Trump told reporters before leaving Washington: “We are going to be talking about denuclearization, a tough subject because it is very hard for any of us to say we’ll get rid of our nukes.” Chinese officials have made clear they are not interested in nuclear talks until their arsenal is roughly the size of the American one.

Mr. Trump also said, “We will talk about Iran a little,” insisting the situation was in “very good shape.” Analysts say it is unlikely that the meeting will make any progress on Iran, with Russia happy to see energy prices elevated since the war began, and China eager to see them rapidly decline.

Protests: Thousands of demonstrators assembled in downtown Manila on Tuesday in opposition to Mr. Trump’s visit and to a U.S.-led initiative called Pax Silica, which aims to unite partners around A.I. supply chains independent of China. Read more ›

North Korea: Mr. Putin could meet with Kim Jong-un if the North Korean leader also travels to China for the APEC summit, Russian state news agencies reported, citing the Kremlin’s spokesman. It was not clear whether Mr. Kim would attend the summit.

Ukraine: Mr. Trump also said he would talk about Ukraine, on which Mr. Putin’s aides are engaging in talks with the U.S. and Ukrainian officials, but also renewing demands that NATO pull back its troops to where they were before the alliance expanded toward Russia.

President Vladimir V. Putin of Russia could meet with Kim Jong-un if the North Korean leader travels to China for the APEC summit this week, the Kremlin said, reflecting the deepening of ties between their countries in recent years.

Asked about the possibility of a meeting, Dmitri S. Peskov, the Kremlin spokesman, said on Monday that it would “of course” happen if Mr. Kim went to Shenzhen, the Chinese economic hub and host city, according to Russian state news agencies. North Korea has not confirmed whether Mr. Kim plans to attend the summit.

Mr. Putin and Mr. Kim have publicly met three times since 2022, and North Korea has been a key supporter of Russia’s military actions in Ukraine.

As the war in Ukraine nears its fifth anniversary, with no end in sight, the Kremlin is expected to use Mr. Putin’s many meetings at the APEC summit to showcase how Russia is regarded as one of the major international powers alongside the United States and China.

A visit by Mr. Kim would be a rare foray outside North Korea for the leader, and it would further raise the prospect that he could meet with President Trump in China. On the first day of his 48-hour trip to Asia, Mr. Trump told reporters that Mr. Kim “would like” to come to the APEC summit. The president has repeatedly said he would like to meet with Mr. Kim again.

On Wednesday, Mr. Putin is scheduled to meet with Xi Jinping, China’s leader. On Thursday, he is set to take part in a trilateral summit with Mr. Xi and Mr. Trump, the first meeting of its kind. The Kremlin said on Monday that it was working to arrange a bilateral meeting between Mr. Putin and Mr. Trump.

Before his departure for China, Mr. Putin met on Monday in the Kremlin with Jo Yong-won, a secretary of North Korea’s ruling Workers’ Party. During the meeting, Mr. Putin said that North Korea’s help in driving Ukrainian forces from the Kursk region of Russia last year “marked a new chapter in the history of military cooperation between our countries.”

Moscow planned to unveil a monument in Kursk by the end of the year to honor the North Korean troops, Mr. Putin said.

During the final meeting of the day, with ASEAN leaders, President Trump told President Ferdinand R. Marcos Jr. of the Philippines:  “I don’t want to date myself, but I knew your parents very well. They were great. I believe in genetics. They did a good job. You have very good genetics, I can tell you that.”

It was not the first time Mr. Trump has praised the family of Mr. Marcos, who is the son of the dictator Ferdinand E. Marcos, who ruled the Philippines for 20 years, imposed martial law and was ousted in 1986.

The only event of substance on President Trump’s schedule today, the signing of a trade deal between the United States and Thailand, did not happen because it was still being finalized. Trump told reporters that “Thailand will eliminate all tariffs on U.S. imports” and that the United States would provide tariff relief, but the two sides signed only a memorandum about the agreement. The president said the deal would be done in the coming weeks.

During an address to Quad leaders, President Trump called Prime Minister Narendra Modi of India, which is a member of the group, “a great leader and a friend of mine, highly respected by everybody all over the world.” The president also praised Modi as he stood with other leaders and took a few questions, calling him a “very tough man.”

India and the United States are navigating a rocky patch in their relationship, largely tied to trade and India’s purchases of Russian oil. Although Modi and Trump signed an interim framework for a trade deal in February, talks have dragged on, frustrating both parties.

The Nvidia turmoil and its implications for the U.S.-China relationship is a backdrop to President Trump’s visit. On Tuesday, shares of technology companies in Asia tumbled after the Trump administration announced rules to restrict overseas access to advanced A.I. chips like those sold by Nvidia. In Hong Kong trading, Alibaba fell 5 percent, while Tencent’s stock dropped nearly 4 percent. The Chinese A.I. firms MiniMax Group fell 7 percent, and Z.AI Co. declined 5 percent.

In the United States, tech stocks stabilized from a sell-off the day before. Nvidia shares were up about 1 percent before the start of regular trading in New York. The company is set to report its latest financial results later on Tuesday.

President Trump is expected to meet with Prime Minister Anutin Charnvirakul of Thailand on Tuesday in Manila to sign what the White House has described as a “trade deal.”

But Thailand’s Ministry of Foreign Affairs said in a statement on Tuesday that Bangkok was expecting to agree only on a “joint statement marking progress in negotiations.”

The dissonance comes amid growing signs within ASEAN, the Association of Southeast Asian Nations, that the United States is not a reliable partner. The meeting between Mr. Trump and Mr. Anutin is taking place on the sidelines of an ASEAN summit.

For more than a year, Washington and Bangkok have been negotiating a new trade agreement that covers items like tariffs, food imports and labor rights.

Last year, the United States battered Thailand with a 36 percent tariff, one of the steepest rates levied by Mr. Trump after his return to office. The United States is one of Thailand’s largest export markets, including for goods like pet food.

Mr. Trump later reduced the tariffs to 19 percent, before the U.S. Supreme Court struck them down in February. The administration eventually found a way to still impose 12.5 percent tariffs on Thai goods.

The signing of a U.S.-Thai trade deal might still be far off, requiring Thailand’s parliament to pass a law, but the meeting delivers an easy publicity win for Mr. Anutin and Mr. Trump, who is known to revel even in perceived success.

Pavida Pananond, an economist at Thammasat Business School in Bangkok, said, “The level of trust Thailand has in the U.S. is not at the same level as before all this economic weaponization of tariffs.”

After handing over the chairmanship of ASEAN to Singapore, President Ferdinand R. Marcos Jr. of the Philippines held a news conference. “President Trump has just arrived,” he said. “He is waiting for us to go to him and to invite him to this really very informal kind of summit.”

Mr. Marcos ended his comments by apologizing for not taking more questions from journalists. “I cannot keep the president of the United States waiting,” he said. “Sorry.”

At his hotel, President Trump sat down for a meeting with Quad leaders and took a few questions. He said that he was “not worried” about bank outages and that the United States would be “looking into” reports of plans by the Iranians to drop a dirty bomb in Washington. He also said that Kim Jong-un, the North Korean leader, would “like to come” to the APEC summit in Shenzhen.

Several hundred activists massed on Tuesday in central Manila to march to the Philippine International Convention Center, the venue for the Association of Southeast Asian Nations summit.

By the time President Trump landed in the city, the ranks of the protesters had swelled to thousands.

Most of the demonstrators were university students who said that they were fed up with the government, which they said was more focused on preserving its relationship with the United States than helping impoverished Filipinos.

Cinna Sison, 18, who studies European languages at the University of the Philippines, said they were inspired by the recent mass protests in France.

“I love the French,” she said. “They know how to protest.”

Ms. Sison said she had worn colorful makeup to mark her first time attending a demonstration. “I am here to show my solidarity with fellow young activists. We are here to show our disgust to the fascist Trump and to Marcos for kowtowing to his every wish,” she said, referring to President Ferdinand Marcos Jr. of the Philippines.

Mr. Trump is expected to hold a bilateral meeting with Mr. Marcos on Wednesday before he heads to China for an Asia-Pacific Economic Cooperation summit.

The Philippine president, who earlier this year described Washington as a “steadfast partner,” has said little publicly about meeting Mr. Trump. His administration has hinted that the two sides are working on a military aid agreement.

In Manila, Ms. Sison wore a red scarf on her head and a red shirt with the words “Silicone not Silica” — a reference to Pax Silica, a U.S.-led consortium of countries aiming to create artificial intelligence supply chains independent of China.

Ms. Sison added that Pax Silica was a symbolic return of the Americans to the Philippines, the site of former U.S. military bases, as Washington leaned on a regional ally to counter China.

“Neighborliness is important, but it is not important to be subservient to U.S. interests,” she said.

Nearby, Raja Bausing, a 22-year-old visual artist, was busy putting the finishing touches on a papier-mâché model of Mr. Trump holding Mr. Marcos.

“We want to show how Trump is a warmonger, while Marcos is a lap dog,” she said. “They are very disrespectful to Filipinos. Trump was welcomed in a grand way and yet we Filipinos are maltreated.”

President Trump landed in Manila on Tuesday, beginning a trip to Asia in which he will attend an ASEAN summit, greet members of the Quad, then travel to China for an APEC meeting.

Here’s what those acronyms mean, as well as some highlights from previous summits — including gold crowns and awkward handshakes.

The Asia-Pacific Economic Cooperation forum is a group of 21 member economies, most of which are from that region. Its members also include Canada, Mexico, Australia, Russia and the United States. It promotes trade in Asia, but is not a free trade bloc. Its leaders meet annually, with the location rotating each year.

Meetings between world leaders on the sidelines of the summit can often overshadow the main proceedings. This year, in the Chinese tech hub of Shenzhen, Mr. Trump is expected to meet with President Xi Jinping of China and President Vladimir V. Putin of Russia, as well as with Mr. Xi separately.

Last year, when Mr. Trump visited the South Korean city of Gyeongju during the week of the APEC meeting, President Lee Jae Myung of South Korea gave him a replica of a gold crown that researchers believe was worn by an ancient Korean ruler. Mr. Trump also met with Mr. Xi in the nearby city of Busan, where the two struck a truce in their countries’ trade war.

A notable incident happened in Sydney, Australia, in 2007, when a group of local television comedians brought an Osama bin Laden look-alike through security. The team came within yards of a hotel where President George W. Bush was staying.

The Association of Southeast Asian Nations is a grouping of 11 countries — Brunei, Cambodia, Indonesia, Laos, Malaysia, Myanmar, the Philippines, Singapore, Thailand, East Timor and Vietnam — which promotes political cooperation and stability across the region. Its leaders meet twice annually.

The bloc was formed in 1967 and represents nearly 700 million people. Its economy and its role in global markets have strengthened in recent years. The United States also sees ASEAN as a geostrategic bulwark against China.

In 2009, an ASEAN meeting in Thailand fell into acrimony when Secretary of State Hillary Clinton compared North Korea’s leaders to “small children” when discussing their nuclear program. The country’s leadership, in turn, described Ms. Clinton as a “schoolgirl” and a “pensioner.”

During the 2017 ASEAN summit in Manila, Mr. Trump grimaced while jostling with Southeast Asian leaders for an awkward cross-armed handshake that was captured in a group photograph.

The Quadrilateral Security Dialogue is an informal partnership between Australia, India, Japan and the United States, which was formed in part to counter China. It was proposed by Prime Minister Shinzo Abe of Japan in 2007.

Beijing opposed the initial discussion, lodging formal diplomatic protests. Australia withdrew in 2008 and the dialogue was put on hold for some time. But the grouping was revived in 2017 on the sidelines of an ASEAN conference.

President Biden was the first U.S. president to convene a meeting among the leaders of the nations as a foursome. In 2024, he hosted the Quad leaders at his home in Wilmington, Del. Mr. Trump has appeared to be less enthusiastic about the partnership. Last year, he did not attend the Quad summit in India.

Reporting from the village of Manabayucan in the Philippines and the capital, Manila

In May, Roseller Rodriguez, a mayor in the Philippines, was startled to learn from news reports that U.S. officials had arrived in his district to start an artificial intelligence hub.

He saw a video of senior American and Filipino officials standing on a makeshift stage, unveiling a new initiative called Pax Silica.

Mr. Rodriguez had not been invited. Nor had he ever heard about the hub. Was it a data center? Where would the water and electricity come from? What would happen to the farmers living there?

Months later, he says he has few answers.

“I’m not concerned with the geopolitics of Pax Silica,” Mr. Rodriguez said in his office in the municipality of Capas. “My problem is Pax Silica will be built in our community.”

The United States started Pax Silica to unite partners around A.I. supply chains independent of China. The initiative is part of President Trump’s effort to counter Beijing’s economic and technological influence.

Trump administration officials have cast it as a 21st-century successor to Pax Americana, the era of relative peace among major powers after World War II.

The significance of Pax Silica, which 26 signatories have joined, has become heightened after revelations that Nvidia’s former chief executive, Jensen Huang, was targeted by Chinese intelligence, exposing the growing geopolitical stakes of the global A.I. race.

In the Philippines, America’s oldest ally in Asia, the backlash has been fierce.

Dozens of Filipino performers greeted President Trump as he descended the stairs of Air Force One onto the tarmac of Ninoy Aquino Airport in Manila, waving flags and playing “Y.M.C.A.” by the Village People on drums and trumpets.

President Vladimir V. Putin could meet with Kim Jong-un if the North Korean leader also travels to China for the APEC summit this week, Russian state news agencies reported, citing Dmitri S. Peskov, the Kremlin’s spokesman. Before his departure for China, Putin met on Monday with a secretary of North Korea’s ruling Workers’ Party. It was not yet clear whether the North Korean leader would attend the summit.

President Trump has landed in Manila, lingering on the tarmac for a welcome ceremony before walking alone to Marine One. He has several events tonight, but he will not leave his hotel for them. A meeting with quad leaders, a U.S.-Thailand trade deal signing and a dinner will all be held at the resort where the president is staying. In other words, he showed up late to this summit, and the summit will be coming to him.

Thousands of protesters have assembled in downtown Manila, chanting in Tagalog, “Trump leave the Philippines.” They are opposed to his Pax Silica A.I. supply chain initiative.

President Trump’s stopover at the Association of Southeast Asian Nations summit is part of a state visit to the Philippines, where he will be received with a 21-gun salute.

The last time he met President Ferdinand R. Marcos Jr. of the Philippines, he was full of praise for the Filipino leader.

“Great family, great family legacy and highly respected in his country,” Mr. Trump said while hosting Mr. Marcos at the White House last year.

But that legacy was long considered an obstacle to Mr. Marcos’s political ambitions. He is the son of the dictator Ferdinand E. Marcos, who ruled the Philippines for 20 years, imposed martial law and was ousted in 1986 by a nonviolent uprising. The elder Marcos died in exile in Hawaii, without facing trial on U.S. criminal charges that he had plundered the Philippine Treasury. Years later, a court found his estate liable for torture, summary executions and disappearances.

The younger Marcos spent years rehabilitating his family’s image and won election in 2022. He is hosting Mr. Trump at his official residence, Malacañang Palace, which was also his childhood home.

Mr. Marcos and his wife, Liza Marcos, will receive Mr. Trump on Wednesday with a 21-gun salute. Mr. Trump is expected to sign the palace guest book, hold closed-door meetings, pose for a handshake, then attend a state luncheon held in his honor.

It is not clear whether Mr. Trump, who has a well-known affinity for autocratic leaders, will meet with Mr. Marcos’s mother, Imelda, 97, with whom he has socialized in New York. Ms. Marcos wielded immense power when her husband was president and later came to be known for her flamboyant lifestyle, shoe collection and unexplained wealth.

Mr. Marcos has said that when he called to congratulate Mr. Trump on his election victory in November 2024, Mr. Trump asked about Imelda Marcos.

“My mother is really his friend, he knows my mother very well,” Mr. Marcos said, relaying the conversation. “He was asking about her, ‘How’s Imelda?’ I told him she sends her congratulations.”

After the pomp and circumstance at Malacañang, which traces its roots to the Spanish colonial era in the Philippines, Mr. Trump is headed to Shenzhen, China. The highlight of that leg of his trip will be a trilateral meeting with two strongmen: President Xi Jinping of China and President Vladimir V. Putin of Russia.

For the second year in a row, President Trump is attending the annual gathering of Southeast Asian leaders, a show of alliance to a region that the United States sees as a strategic and maritime counterweight to China.

But Mr. Trump reached the gathering, held this year in Manila, only on Tuesday, just as the summit was wrapping up, and he is not expected to make any significant policies or diplomacy moves.

Mixed signals like this — to say nothing of Mr. Trump’s punishing tariffs — are adding to a widespread belief within ASEAN, the Association of Southeast Asian Nations, that the United States is not a reliable partner. That leaves the bloc balancing between Washington and Beijing, with which many of its members have deep trade partnerships.

“They’ve learned that the United States is not reliable, so a lot of the hedging has been away from the United States,” said Ted Osius, a former U.S. ambassador to Vietnam.

The Philippines, America’s oldest security ally in Asia, provides a window into this balancing act.

After taking office three years ago, President Ferdinand R. Marcos Jr. reversed his predecessor’s tilt toward China and pivoted back toward the United States. That stance contributed to many tense encounters between Filipino and Chinese vessels in the South China Sea, where Manila and Beijing have competing territorial claims.

As U.S. patrols in the South China Sea have dropped, Mr. Marcos has appeared to be more conciliatory toward Beijing. In August, he said that there was a “distinct possibility” that the Philippines and China could partner on oil and gas exploration in those waters despite their territorial feud.

“Marcos Jr. already has begun a shift toward China, one that a single visit by Trump is unlikely to stop,” Joshua Kurlantzick, a senior fellow for Southeast Asia and South Asia at the Council on Foreign Relations, wrote last week.

On Sunday, China and ASEAN failed to agree on a code of conduct in the South China Sea — something the Philippines, which has some of the biggest stakes in the dispute, had vowed to secure while chairing the bloc this year.

Chester Cabalza, president of International Development and Security Cooperation, a Philippine think tank, said: “Strategically, Manila opens its doors for conciliation with Beijing to stabilize normal ties. But on the ground, the real battle has yet to subside.”

Domestically, Mr. Marcos has to walk a tightrope, too.

“For Filipino fishermen and coastal communities, the South China Sea is not simply about a geopolitical dispute. It concerns livelihoods, access to maritime resources and national dignity,” said Arnaud Leveau, president of the Asia Centre, a Paris-based think tank. Any diplomatic accommodation perceived as sacrificing Filipino interests, he said, would therefore be politically risky.

The wider region has been battered by the consequences of another Trump policy: the war in Iran. Southeast Asia depends heavily on oil and gas imports from the Middle East and was roiled by soaring prices of fuel. In the Philippines, crops rotted in fields because farmers could not afford to transport them to markets. In Thailand, public servants were asked to use stairs instead of elevators to reduce energy expenses.

ASEAN comprises 11 nations — Brunei, Cambodia, Indonesia, Laos, Malaysia, Myanmar, the Philippines, Singapore, Thailand, Vietnam and, since last year, East Timor — with vastly different degrees of freedom and wealth. The bloc represents almost 700 million people, or almost 10 percent of the global population.

Making decisions on a consensus-basis and pledging not to interfere in one other’s internal affairs, the bloc has often been criticized as slow and ineffective, with the situation in Myanmar often cited as an example. A coup in that country in 2021 unleashed a civil war that ranks among the world’s most violent conflicts.

Myanmar’s leaders remain barred from ASEAN summits, under an agreement that was extended on Monday. Still, almost half of the bloc’s member states have had bilateral engagements with Myanmar since the coup leader, Min Aung Hlaing, used stage-managed elections to take the post of president this year. Washington has also recently re-engaged in direct talks with Myanmar.

Mr. Trump’s attendance at the summit, which concludes with an ASEAN-U.S. meeting, is part of a state visit to the Philippines. He is expected to hold a bilateral meeting with Mr. Marcos on Wednesday before heading to China for an Asia-Pacific Economic Cooperation summit. Mr. Marcos has said little publicly about meeting Mr. Trump; but earlier this year, he described Washington as a “steadfast partner.” His administration has hinted that the two sides are working on a military aid agreement.

In recent days, hundreds of demonstrators have gathered in Manila to protest Mr. Trump’s visit. They oppose his “Pax Silica” initiative, a move to build a supply chain for the artificial intelligence industry that bypasses China, saying it would exploit Philippine natural resources.

Renato Reyes, who led one of the protest groups on Sunday afternoon, said, “Marcos is so desperate to welcome Donald Trump that he will roll out the red carpet, even if Trump arrives late for the summit.”

Aie Balagtas See contributed reporting from Manila, and Damien Cave from Ho Chi Minh City, Vietnam.

President Trump sets off this week for meetings in Asia that will culminate in a summit with two strongmen he has often praised: President Xi Jinping of China and President Vladimir V. Putin of Russia.

It will be the first time the three men, who command the world’s largest nuclear arsenals, will have sat down together. Mr. Trump plans to discuss the reduction of nuclear weapons in the world and the war against Iran, according to a White House spokeswoman. He is not expected to bring up Russia’s war with Ukraine.

Mr. Trump will travel first to Manila, then to southern China, and will meet with regional leaders confronting an array of uncertainties. Those include the course of the war between the United States and Iran, high energy prices and mounting concern over the risks of artificial intelligence.

For Mr. Trump, the trip will be a pivot to foreign policy and relationships after American voters delivered a swift rebuke to his party in the midterm elections. His trip also comes as his administration faces scrutiny over a scandal involving the former chief executive of Nvidia, the tech giant, which has raised questions about Chinese espionage and American tech exports.

The highlight of Mr. Trump’s trip is expected to be his meeting with Mr. Xi and Mr. Putin on Thursday in China.

The prospect of their summit has drawn comparisons to the 1945 conference in the Crimean city of Yalta, where the leaders of the United States, the Soviet Union and Britain met to decide the shape of postwar Europe.

The White House has said that Mr. Trump wants to discuss the threat of nuclear proliferation. U.S. officials have expressed concerns about China’s accelerating nuclear buildup, expected to reach 1,000 nuclear warheads by 2030.

The last nuclear arms control treaty between the United States and Russia expired in February. Trump administration officials have said that any new agreement on nuclear arms control must include China, but Beijing has shown no interest in the topic. China has an estimated 620 nuclear warheads, compared with the more than 5,000 that Russia and the United States have each, according to the Stockholm International Peace Research Institute.

U.S. administration officials said they did not expect a deal out of the meeting, which they characterized as a starting point for discussions.

The gathering has prompted concern in Ukraine that Mr. Putin might push for a deal, deciding Ukraine’s fate without Kyiv’s involvement. A U.S. administration official said Ukraine was not expected to be part of the discussion between the three leaders.

Mr. Trump will start his trip in the Philippines, the oldest American treaty ally in the Asia-Pacific region, as the United States faces questions about its support for its partners in a region where China’s military and economic might continues to grow.

In Manila, President Ferdinand R. Marcos Jr. plans to discuss with Mr. Trump the South China Sea, where the Philippines and China have competing claims. Philippine officials have said that they hope Mr. Trump’s visit might result in more American military assistance.

Mr. Trump is also expected to meet with leaders of ASEAN, a Southeast Asian grouping of 11 nations, as well as a gathering of the so-called Quad grouping of the United States, India, Australia and Japan.

Prime Minister Anthony Albanese of Australia said on Sunday that he expected “lots of engagement” with Mr. Trump during the meetings.

“Obviously, that’s our most important ally,” he said. “But we want to see something constructive come out of that, including we want to see a de-escalation in the conflicts that are around the world.”

Chinese analysts described the meeting of the Quad group, formed in part to counter China, as an effort by Mr. Trump to project power after his party’s defeat in the midterms. “Its symbolic value outweighs its practical value,” Xu Liping, a senior scholar at Guangdong University of Foreign Studies, told Shanghai Observer, a state-run outlet.

After Manila, Mr. Trump will travel to the Chinese tech hub of Shenzhen, where he will meet Mr. Xi on the sidelines of the Asia-Pacific Economic Cooperation.

Jensen Huang, a key adviser who accompanied Mr. Trump on his last trip to China, will not be by his side. Mr. Huang helped shape the president’s views about technological competition with China and persuaded him to loosen restrictions on selling advanced chips to China.

Mr. Huang stepped down from his role at Nvidia after revelations that he had an affair with a Chinese businesswoman, Alice Huang, who U.S. intelligence officials believe was collecting intelligence on Mr. Huang for Beijing. China has rejected the allegations, calling them “smears.” Ms. Huang has not responded to requests for comment.

So far, the president has not raised the issue with Mr. Xi and is not expected to during their meeting on Thursday. But the saga is likely to cause unease in China about whether Washington will impose tighter controls on the export of technology to the country.

As a result, U.S.-based analysts speculated that Mr. Xi might try to use his personal relationship with Mr. Trump to forestall such restrictions.

Yun Sun, director of the China program at the Stimson Center in Washington, said Mr. Huang’s downfall would likely mean “there is going to be more anxiety on the Chinese side about their access to American chips.” She expects Mr. Xi to emphasize the importance of easing U.S. controls on chip exports to China during their meeting.

Cooperation between China and the United States on artificial intelligence may be more difficult after the Nvidia scandal.

Still, there may be progress on the creation of a notification system that would allow the countries to alert each other about national security issues related to artificial intelligence, which the two sides agreed to in September.

“There’s some shared concern on both sides that U.S. or Chinese models might be misused by bad actors and cause damage to the U.S., China, or other countries,” said Kyle Chan, a fellow at the Brookings Institution focusing on China’s technology development.

When Mr. Xi sits down with Mr. Trump in Shenzhen, he will be dealing with a president who will soon face a new constraint at home: a Democrat-controlled Congress.

Lawmakers may pressure Mr. Trump to tighten export controls against China or accelerate weapons sales to Taiwan, according to Ali Wyne, a senior research and advocacy adviser focusing on U.S.-China relations at the International Crisis Group. That means that “Xi will have to work much harder to keep Trump on side,” Mr. Wyne said.

Mr. Trump will be the first American president to visit China twice in one calendar year.

Analysts say the leaders’ summits, which have included state visits in Beijing and then in Washington, have been the only thing holding together a fragile détente between the two countries as they clash over A.I., export controls, Taiwan and other issues.

The two sides declared a trade truce last year when they met in Busan, South Korea. That deal, which was to expire this month, has been extended until January.

China’s restrictions on the export of crucial rare-earth metals and rare-earth magnets, which are needed to manufacture most modern technology, remain one of the most difficult unresolved issues.

Mr. Trump is likely to raise this with Mr. Xi, according to a senior U.S. administration official who said that China’s continued restrictions is one reason Washington only agreed to a two-month extension of the trade truce.

Ana Swanson contributed reporting from Washington, Aie Balagtas See from Manila, and Laura Chung from Sydney, Australia.

The spread of antimicrobial resistance (AMR) is threatening modern medicine despite progress in combating drug-resistant bacteria, senior World Health Organization officials said Monday. WHO officials said countries had expanded efforts to tackle AMR, but warned that progress in prevention, diagnosis and the appropriate use of antibiotics remained too slow to meet global targets. #13. Lewis Hamilton visits Egypt’s Rafah crossing and Gaza aid facilities

Seven-time F1 world champion tours border area and logistics warehouses operated by Egyptian Red Crescent

Lewis Hamilton has visited Egypt’s Rafah border crossing with the Gaza Strip to meet organisations sending humanitarian aid to the territory.

The seven-time Formula One world champion has spoken out about conflict and humanitarian crises in the region, and has called for peace. He has frequently posted on social media about children killed in Israeli airstrikes in Gaza and called for a ceasefire.

“I think we need to make sure we’re pushing to free Congo, free Sudan, free Syria, free Palestine. I think there’s a lot of peace that I’m hoping we get to at some stage,” Hamilton said in October.

A ceasefire agreement reached last year has halted major combat operations in Gaza, but the amount of aid reaching the territory continues to fall far short of the agreed-upon minimum of 600 trucks a day.

Israeli forces have continued to advance, expanding their control from 53% of the territory to at least 60%, an independent commission of UN experts said last month.

Airstrikes also continue, and Israeli fire along the advancing front has killed at least 196 Palestinians, including 18 women, 32 boys and 11 girls, the commission said.

Hamilton toured Egypt’s Rafah border crossing with Gaza before visiting logistics warehouses operated by the Egyptian Red Crescent, which facilitates the entry of aid to Gaza.

At the crossing, Hamilton greeted aid workers with “Assalamu alaikum” a widely used Arabic greeting meaning “peace be upon you.” He shook hands and briefly spoke to workers and officials.

Khaled Megawer, the governor of North Sinai, where Rafah is located, said the visit was intended to help Hamilton learn about Egypt’s efforts to send aid to Gaza.

The visit comes after Hamilton made his 400th race start on Sunday at the São Paulo Grand Prix at Interlagos.

The first black F1 driver, he made his debut with McLaren in 2007. He is only the second F1 driver to start 400 races after his former teammate Fernando Alonso, who achieved the milestone in 2024.

#12. Thomas Markle charged over child sex abuse material in Philippines

Thomas Markle has also been charged in the US

Police in the Philippines have charged Thomas Markle Sr, the father of Meghan, Duchess of Sussex, with possession of and intent to distribute child sexual abuse material.

The charges were filed on Monday, Cebu province prosecutor Graeme Elmido told the BBC.

Earlier this month, Markle, 82, was separately arrested and charged in the US with possession of child sexual abuse material. The FBI said agents, after searching storage units Markle rented, seized about 78 items, including film negatives, slides and books, that they allege contained child sex abuse material.

Markle, who is in custody in the US, has previously denied the allegations. The BBC has contacted Markle's attorney for comment.

Elmido told the BBC that Philippine National Police had charged Markle Sr with "two counts of intent to distribute CSAEM [child sexual abuse and exploitation material], and one count of possession of CSAEM material".

Last month, police in the Philippines obtained a warrant to examine devices allegedly removed from Markle's former home in the country.

Four boxes of material, including computers, phones, hard drives and old video tapes, were handed to officers by Meghan's half brother Thomas Markle Jr.

Earlier this month, Markle Sr was arrested and charged in the US. He had initially been placed under house detention at his assisted living facility in Florida, but was taken back into custody last week after prosecutors alleged he removed his ankle monitor.

Meghan Markle has been estranged from her father since 2018. The BBC understands she broke contact with him last year, telling him she and the authorities knew he had abused young girls.

In a previously shared statement after the allegations against her father emerged, Meghan wrote: "I am deeply distressed... my thoughts are with any children who may have been harmed."

She said she hoped it could be understood why she had largely cut off contact from her father and did "not allow him to have contact with our children".

#10. Nvidia says it won't reinstate Huang as firm reports bumper results

Jensen Huang and Nvidia's interim CEO, Colette Kress

Nvidia's quarterly sales doubled to top $100bn for the first time as the company insisted it is not reinstating ousted co-founder Jensen Huang.

In the company's first results since Huang's dramatic exit, interim chief executive Colette Kress said it is "business as usual" and told investors: "We've got this."

The company's quarterly sales doubled from a year earlier, reaching $115.8bn (Â£86.9bn), with shares in the company jumping by almost 4% in after-hours trading.

Earlier on Tuesday, Huang said he would be "honoured" to return and lead Nvidia, having quit following two alleged extramarital affairs, including one with a suspected spy for China.

"The board does not intend to reinstate him," Nvidia chair Harvey Jones said during a Tuesday call with financial analysts.

The company's profits also doubled from a year ago. For the quarter, which covered the three months to October 25, profit hit $65bn. Its revenue from data centres, a significant part of Nvidia's business, also doubled to $107bn in the same time period.

Going forward, the company said its rapid growth is poised to continue, and revenue will rise another 13% in the next quarter.

Watch: Who is suspected Chinese spy at centre of Nvidia affair investigation?

Given the uncertainty surrounding the company after Huang's exit, financial analysts had been unsure Nvidia would provide any forward-looking guidance.

Daniela Hathorn, a senior analyst for trading platform Capital.com, said Nvidia's results "offer a strong answer to concerns that demand for AI infrastructure is cooling" and temper immediate worry that Huang was irreplaceable as a leader.

"The harder question is whether Nvidia can preserve the strategic advantage Huang helped build," Hathorn added. "His exit is unlikely to change orders already committed, but could matter more when Nvidia makes its next round of decisions on products, partnerships and investment."

Kress seemed eager to stress that Nvidia can continue to not only succeed, but lead the ongoing AI buildout happening throughout the technology industry.

"It is business as usual," she said of what is happening inside of Nvidia now. "Our partners, our suppliers, all of them are 100% behind us."

She added that Nvidia's leadership team has "stood up very strongly over the last week... working seamlessly in all of their departments and projects".

"Our momentum is real. Our demand is accelerating. Our supply is growing. Our customers and our partners are with us, and employees are committed." Kress said before leaving the analyst call. "We've got this."

The rise and fall of Jensen Huang - how the tech superstar became mired in scandal

Jensen Huang says he would be 'honoured' to return as Nvidia CEO if board asked him

Despite board chairman Jones saying earlier that the company would not answer analyst questions about Huang or Nvidia's investigation into his conduct, he did address the search for a permanent replacement.

Jones said Nvidia is working with an executive search firm and is considering internal and external candidates, but that the company would not rush its selection.

"With the advanced technology and the national security dynamics in the marketplace, our new CEO will have to navigate those waters carefully," Jones said. "The search will be thorough and we want to do it as quickly as possible, but we won't do it before we find the exact right person."

Michael Monaghan from investment firm Founder ETFs is among those who want Huang back in the top job at Nvidia.

The Nvidia investor told the BBC that he is waiting to see the results of a thorough investigation into Huang's actions, instead of relying on "salacious allegations" made in the media.

Nvidia's strong earnings are "from the machine that Jensen built" and a company tends to lose "its shine and vision" after an iconic founder leaves, Monaghan said.

#9. Nvidia board rules that co-founder Jensen Huang will not return as CEO

Nvidia's board chairman ruled out bringing back co-founder Jensen Huang as CEO on Tuesday, as the chipmaker forecast stronger-than-expected quarterly sales but faced growing questions over its leadership, national security exposure and long-term strategy following his abrupt departure.

#8. Zimbabwean author Simbarashe Kundizeza cleared of using AI to write prize-winning story

After a month-long investigation it was concluded that Kundizeza’s story The Blue Fighter, which won the £10,000 Caine prize, was ‘human-written’

The Zimbabwean author Simbarashe Kundizeza has been cleared of using AI to write a prize-winning short story, after the prestigious Caine prize for African writers concluded that his entry was “human-written” after a month-long investigation.

Kundizeza’s story The Blue Fighter, which charts the declining fortunes of a family after the father invests in fish tanks, was announced as the winner of the £10,000 prize on 6 October.

Five days later, the Atlantic published an article titled “Yet another literary prize winner seems to be AI”, in which the author and journalist Vauhini Vara said the AI-detection software Pangram, which itself uses AI, had deemed the story to be almost completely artificially generated.

The Caine prize chair, Ellah Wakatama, said in a statement: “From our detailed investigation, we feel the evidence is clear: The Blue Fighter was human-written. We now look forward to awarding and celebrating Simbarashe Steyn Kundizeza … before the end of the year.”

She said: “When concerns were raised, we have a duty as an organisation to look at them carefully and fairly,” adding that the prize had taken a “human-centred” approach to its investigation.

“Our review included interviews with the author, the 2026 judging panel and The Shallow Tales Review, where the story was first published,” Wakatama said. “It also included independent and expert-led metadata analysis of drafts written by the author.”

Kundizeza, who had strongly denied using generative AI to write the story, said he was “relieved” that the allegations had been dismissed after the investigation. “It was not necessary in the first place, I am happy to put all this behind me and continue with my writing journey,” he said.

Vara’s article prompted outrage online, with many claiming that Black writers, and Africans in particular, were being unfairly singled out by allegations of the use of AI.

Associated Press contributed to this report

#6. How the attack on Crimean Bridge unfolded

Footage shows Ukrainian missile approach Crimea bridge

Stretching across the Kerch Strait in the Black Sea, the Crimean Bridge was always a symbol: for Russia, a prestige project cementing its claim to sovereign Ukrainian territory, and for Ukraine, a hated piece of infrastructure emblematic of Moscow's occupation of the Crimean Peninsula.

A key section of the double-arched, 12-mile-long bridge now lies on the seabed, brought down by an arsenal of Ukrainian long-range weaponry in a seven-stage operation culminating in a dramatic collapse.

Kremlin spokesman Dmitry Peskov has said that the bridge "will be rebuilt, and it will be even better than before".

But eight years after its unveiling, the bridge has now become material proof of Kyiv's ability to pierce through Moscow's military defences to hit even its most treasured and protected structures.

The attack on the strategic Crimean Bridge was carried out gradually to cut across several layers of defence, Kyiv confirmed on Tuesday.

For several weeks Ukraine's Security Service and its General Staff gradually destroyed air defence systems, radars, electronic warfare assets, and other designated targets, President Volodymyr Zelensky said.

A significant strike had already been reported on 16 November, when drones hit the bridge at night. Traffic was paused briefly then reinstated, and although the arch over the road section was visibly damaged, the structure remained standing.

But that hit turned out to have been only the penultimate stage of a complex attack.

In the early hours of 17 November, the final strikes dealt a fatal blow to the weakened arch structure.

One verified video - filmed from a camera on board a Ukrainian-made Flamingo missile - shows the weapon approaching the main span of the bridge and heading towards the steel arches, with the footage freezing just before impact.

The Flamingo had potentially been modified to be controlled remotely, which improved its accuracy enough to strike the bridge supports, munitions expert Justin Bronk, from the Royal United Services Institute said.

The missile's "very heavy warhead" made it "more suitable" for attacking the bridge's reinforced concrete spans and supports, Bronk added.

Zelensky later confirmed that several types of missiles and drones had been employed in the attack, which led the massive road arch - weighing thousands of tonnes and measuring about 227m in length - to collapse into the strait.

Daylight images taken from the shores of Crimea to the north show a section about 200m (660ft) long of the tarmac was too dragged below into the sea.

This is clearly critical damage to the crossing. Far from just being a decorative feature of the bridge, the arch played a structural role, transferring loads and supporting the span and helping to ensure the stability of the entire structure.

The parallel arch supporting the railway section has remained standing, although it may also have been damaged. All traffic on the bridge was initially suspended, though trains were later allowed to resume running.

But the damage to the bridge's main span is "catastrophic", Colin Capriani, a structural engineer from Monash University in Melbourne, Australia, told BBC Verify. It was "very hard to envisage" how the bridge could be rebuilt given it is clearly well within range of Ukrainian weaponry, Capriani added.

Major attacks in 2022 and 2023 had already caused serious damage to the road spans of the bridge, but the structure survived.

The difference this time is "the most critical and vulnerable part of the bridge has been hit", said David MacKenzie of engineering consultancy COWI, adding that "although the wreckage of the bridge can be cleared fairly quickly, reinstating full road traffic will take over a year".

Moscow uses Crimea as a military base, with airbases, troop concentrations, training grounds and artillery stockpiles. From there it also carries out strikes on the rest of the country and resupplies its troops in southern Ukraine.

The attack comes after a summer of frequent strikes on Crimea, dictated by Ukraine's stated goal of cutting the peninsula off from Russia. At the time, Kyiv vowed to turn the Crimean Peninsula "into an island".

Now - although it is too early to speak of a complete blockade of Crimea â the risk of a serious transport and logistics crisis has increased significantly.

There is still a land corridor running through the occupied parts of the Zaporizhzhia and Kherson regions. However, Ukraine has been systematically striking transport routes across northern Crimea, as well as bridges and other crossings in the area around Chongar, which has already caused problems with the delivery of fuel and other supplies, including military cargo.

Moscow can also still utilise the ferry crossing across the Kerch Strait. It could partly compensate for the loss of the bridge, but its capacity is very limited - and anything on the Black Sea has become vulnerable to attack. Resupplying Crimea by sea is risky, as Ukrainian naval and aerial drones operate extensively in the area.

In addition, debris from the Crimean Bridge - particularly its enormous arch - may have blocked the shipping channel. This would make it harder for larger vessels to pass through and create further problems for ports in the Sea of Azov, including the occupied cities of Mariupol and Berdyansk.

Away from the operational value of the damage to the bridge, the symbolic significance remains.

In 2018, Putin triumphantly drove over the bridge representing the unification of Russia and Crimea in a Russian-made truck. That section of the road now lies on the seabed.

Nearly five years since he launched a full-scale invasion of Ukraine, Putin continues to wreak destruction on Ukraine's civilian population, but his most prized symbols of power are increasingly threatened by Kyiv's reach.

#5. Relocation of Cape Town baboons to sanctuary ignites heated protests

Demonstrators tried to block vehicle carrying the baboons, whose capture authorities say is needed to protect both primate and human

The relocation of a troop of baboons in Cape Town to a wildlife sanctuary has triggered furious protests by local people opposed to their capture, which officials argue is necessary to stop human-wildlife conflict in the city.

The 44-member Waterfall troop (named after the area they mainly inhabit) was captured in the southern seaside suburb of Simon’s Town on Friday and Saturday. A second, smaller Seaforth troop is also due to be moved.

The growth in both baboon and human populations in Cape Town in recent years has led to increased conflict and more baboons dying as a result. Meanwhile, a heated debate has raged between people who believe that baboons and humans can coexist and those who argue that baboons in urban areas are in danger and in some cases need to be relocated. Some animal rights activists have compared authorities’ baboon management to apartheid.

During Friday’s relocation, outraged protesters tried to stop vehicles taking the baboons away. One person sprayed a fire extinguisher, according to the Daily Maverick, a local news outlet.

After the confrontation, the local Society for the Prevention of Cruelty to Animals branch urged those opposed to the baboons’ capture not to try to stop the vehicles. “Capture and transport are already stressful for the baboons,” it said in a statement. “Delaying their arrival prolongs their time in crates and risks adding to that stress – this is not fair on the baboons.”

It said it remained opposed to the baboon’s relocation, adding: “Our presence did not imply support … our inspectors are present because the animals need someone looking out for them.”

On Sunday, dozens of people sat outside the wildlife centre in a silent protest, some tying flowers to the fence. Baboons from a different troop also gathered at the boundary, calling to the newly captive animals, the Daily Maverick reported.

“We stand together for the Seaforth and Waterfall troops, for their freedom, their dignity and their right to live as wild animals,” Mandy Neal, the administrator of the Save Kogelfontein Baboons Facebook group, posted on Monday, calling for weekly Sunday silent protests. “ Our message is simple: CAPTIVITY IS NOT CONSERVATION.”

The City of Cape Town said in a statement on Sunday that the relocation had been successful, with the baboons being monitored 24/7 after being checked by vets and the females receiving contraceptive implants.

It said: “The Waterfall troop has been relocated due to the severity and persistence of human-baboon conflict in the Simon’s Town urban environment, the limited available natural low-lying habitat, the increasing risk to the baboons’ welfare, and public safety concerns … [and] as a very last resort after other interventions were evaluated and deemed ineffective, and as an alternative to culling.”

Prof Justin O’Riain, a behavioural ecologist at the University of Cape Town, said the baboon management plan had undergone two years of public consultations. “It has thus been disappointing watching members of the public and select NGOs, many which did not engage in the stakeholder process, now compromising the safety and welfare of the baboons and people through violent actions directed at staff and baboons in transit.”

#4. Putin's Crimean Bridge partly collapses as Zelensky hails 'unprecedented' attack by Ukraine

Footage shows Ukrainian missile approach Crimea bridge

Ukraine has severely damaged a critical bridge linking Russia to occupied Crimea, in what President Volodymyr Zelensky says was an "unprecedented operation" weeks in the making.

Images shared by Ukrainian officials show a section of the Crimean Bridge collapsed into the water, dealing a logistical and symbolic blow to Moscow's war effort.

The Kremlin said it would rebuild the bridge "better than before".

Meanwhile in Kyiv, Mayor Vitaliy Klitschko said traffic had partially resumed on the Pivnichnyi (Northern) and Pivdennyi (Southern) bridges - key crossings over the Dnipro River - after they had been closed following Russian strikes overnight.

Ukraine has long targeted the Crimean Bridge, which provides a road and rail route for Russian soldiers heading towards the front line, but has struggled to close it for good.

While Kyiv has been trying to increase the cost to Russia of waging war economically, by hitting oil refineries and depots, striking the bridge is an attempt to increase the political cost on Russian President Vladimir Putin.

The timing of the strike comes at a significant moment, ahead of Putin meeting the leaders of China and the US. This could cloud his moment back in the spotlight.

The Crimean Bridge was built by Russia after it invaded and illegally annexed Ukraine's southern peninsula in 2014.

The $3.6bn (Â£2.7bn) piece of infrastructure is widely seen in Russia and Ukraine as Putin's personal pet project: His close tycoon friend Arkady Rotenberg built it, and then Putin personally opened it by driving a lorry over it in May 2018.

Putin (centre) personally opened the Crimean Bridge by driving a lorry over it in May 2018

In Ukraine, the much-hated bridge is seen as one of the main symbols of Russia's continuing occupation of Ukrainian lands.

"We're drinking champagne!" Andrii, an engineer who lives in Kyiv, told the BBC.

He said the latest attack would "deal a very heavy blow to the morale of the Russians".

Some Ukrainians are concerned that Putin might now respond by carrying out even more attacks against civilian infrastructure, but Andrii said: "I don't think the Russians can make things much worse; they're already pounding us every single night with everything they've got."

Images show the damage to the Crimean Bridge from the overnight attack

The damage after the overnight attack, involving cruise missiles and drones, appeared to be far more severe than during previous strikes on the bridge, with a large arched section apparently destroyed.

Video footage appeared to show critical damage to the road section, with remnants of the support arch and road deck seen protruding from the water below.

Zelensky said the strike was the result of a seven-stage operation by the military and security services, which saw Ukrainian forces "gradually destroy air defence systems, radars, electronic warfare equipment, and other designated targets on the way to the final objective".

He said several types of Ukrainian drones as well as the country's Flamingo and Neptune cruise missiles were fired overnight during a "pivotal stage".

The Ukrainian president also said the rail section parallel to the bridge's road had also been hit by a wave of drones, though images showed its supporting arch was still standing and Russia's transport ministry later said some rail services resumed.

Ukraine's state security service SBU said its operations had "systematically suppressed enemy air defence systems" over a month, and said it showed "no Russian defence systems can guarantee the safety of illegal objects occupied by the invaders".

Meanwhile, Ukraine's military said the roadway on the bridge is "out of action", with no vehicle able to cross it, and the railway section is still standing, but with a damaged arch.

The Kremlin said Putin had been briefed on the overnight attack. Kremlin spokesman Dmitry Peskov said the bridge would "be restored following Ukrainian strikes, and it will be even better than before", and did not rule out the possibility the strikes were linked to Putin's visit to China.

Russia's Investigative Committee confirmed structural damage and said it had opened a "criminal case... on terrorism charges" after attacks by "Ukraine's armed formations".

For the second day in a row, Russia recorded the biggest Ukrainian drone attack since the war began, with 2,256 drones downed, according to the defence ministry.

Zelensky had warned of major attacks in the days leading up to a meeting between Putin, Donald Trump and Xi Jinping in China's Shenzhen on Thursday.

Watch: BBC reports from Kyiv bridge hit by Russian attack

In Kyiv, loud explosions were heard just before midnight on Monday, with Klitschko saying shortly after that the city had suffered "damage to critical infrastructure systems" and disruption to heating and water supplies.

On Tuesday, Zelensky said four ballistic missiles had targeted the Southern Bridge, and another three had been fired on the Northern Bridge.

Some of Kyiv's above-ground metro services were also damaged in the overnight attacks, local officials said, after civilians were told to go to civil defence shelters.

Eight major bridges span the Dnipro River, connecting the western and eastern parts of Kyiv, and an estimated three million residents rely on the bridges to travel across the city.

Elsewhere, three people have been injured in a Russian missile attack in the central Cherkasy region, according to local officials.

The long-term impact of the Crimean Bridge strike is still not fully clear, though the risk of a serious transport and logistics crisis for the Russian military has increased significantly.

The collapse of the arched section has cut off the peninsula's direct road and rail links with Russia â at least until the crossing can be repaired.

However, there is still a land corridor running through the occupied parts of Ukraine's southern Zaporizhzhia and Kherson regions - though Kyiv has been systematically striking transport routes across northern Crimea, as well as bridges and other crossings in the area around Chongar.

This has already caused problems with the delivery of fuel and other supplies, including military cargo.

There is a ferry crossing across the Kerch Strait, which connects the Black Sea with the Sea of Azov. It could partly compensate for the loss of the bridge, but its capacity is very limited and it is also vulnerable to attack.

Russia could also supply Crimea by sea, but this route is also risky, as Ukrainian naval and aerial drones operate extensively in the area.

In addition, debris from the Crimean Bridge â particularly its enormous arch â may have blocked the shipping channel.

This would make it harder for larger vessels to pass through and create further problems for the Russian-occupied ports in the Sea of Azov, including Mariupol and Berdyansk.

Russia launched its full-scale invasion of Ukraine in February 2022. Approximately one-fifth of Ukraine is now under Russian occupation and Kyiv has refused Putin's demand to give up areas of Ukraine's eastern Donbas that remain under its control.

#3. US basketball player arrested with 75lb of marijuana at Nigerian airport, say authorities

Drug smuggling carries life sentence in Nigeria

An American basketball player has been arrested in Nigeria for allegedly attempting to smuggle 33.95kg (75lb) of marijuana into the country.

Nigeria’s National Drug Law Enforcement Agency (NDLEA) said a 28-year-old player named as Greene Da’monte Khiree from the state of Georgia was arrested at Lagos’s main airport last Wednesday after he arrived on a flight from Thailand via the United Arab Emirates.

The NDLEA said the player told them he had come to Nigeria to “build networks” for his basketball career. The agency said evidence on his phone suggested he had “knowledge of and involvement in the drug trafficking activity”. The NDLEA said it detained the player when he attempted to leave the airport with his hand luggage after abandoning two checked-in bags. The agency said those two bags were found to contain 32 parcels of marijuana, and a tracking device.

Nigeria has strict drug laws, and trafficking carries a sentence of life in prison.

A basketball player by the name of Damonte Greene is listed on the roster of the Pattani Raptors in the Thai Premier Men’s League. Greene, a Georgia native, played college basketball for Reinhardt University and South Georgia Technical College. He played semi-professional basketball in the US before representing a number of teams in Thailand.

WNBA star Brittney Griner and former Utah State player Jarred Shaw were imprisoned in Russia and Indonesia respectively for marijuana offenses. Griner was released in December 2022, while Shaw remains in prison.

#2. Trump pleads not guilty to 34 felony counts

Our live coverage of Trump’s historic indictment has moved.

Acting New York Supreme Court Judge Juan Merchan wrote his Monday night order that the media’s request to broadcast the arraignment of former President Donald Trump was understandable, but that the news organizations’ interests in providing the broadest possible access to the proceedings must be weighed against “competing interests.”

In rejecting the request to broadcast the arraignment live on television, Merchan still wrote about the historical significance of the proceeding in stark terms.

“That this indictment involves a matter of monumental significance cannot possibly be disputed. Never in the history of the United States has a sitting or past President been indicted on criminal charges. Mr. Trump’s arraignment has generated unparalleled public interest and media attention,” he wrote. “The populace rightly hungers for the most accurate and current information available. To suggest otherwise would be disingenuous.”

News outlets will not be allowed to broadcast former President Donald Trump’s arraignment on Tuesday in a New York state court, a judge said Monday night, but he will allow some photographers to take pictures in the courtroom before the proceedings formally begin.

Acting New York Supreme Court Judge Juan Merchan rejected the request by several media organizations, including CNN, for permission to broadcast the historic proceedings. Trump’s arraignment — like most arraignments in the Manhattan courthouse — is a public proceeding, but news cameras are not usually allowed to broadcast from inside the courtroom.

However, the judge is allowing five pool photographers to take still photos at the beginning of the proceedings “until such time as they are directed to vacate the jury box by court personnel.”

Earlier on Monday, Trump’s lawyers urged the judge to reject the media’s request for live cameras in the courtroom. The Manhattan District Attorney’s office told the judge they didn’t have a position.

The media outlets that tried to get cameras into the courtroom argued that “the gravity of this proceeding … and, consequently, the need for the broadest possible public access, cannot be overstated.”

Trump is now in Manhattan ahead of the arraignment. A grand jury indicted the former president last week.

The arraignment is also expected to bring the unsealing of the criminal charges against Trump, which have not yet been seen by his lawyers or the public.

The indictment stems from Manhattan District Attorney Alvin Bragg’s investigation into hush-money payments, made during the 2016 presidential campaign, to women who claimed they had extramarital affairs with Trump, which he denies.

Trump denies all wrongdoing and his lawyers said last week that they’ll fight to get the charges dropped.

Donald Trump is not planning to speak publicly in New York on Tuesday, aides say, if he follows advice from members of his legal team who have urged the former President to refrain from the vitriol he’s been directing toward the prosecutor and judge presiding over the case.

But that restraint does not extend to Florida, aides said, where he is planning to deliver a full-throated defense of his New York case as well as other investigations he is facing during a prime time address from Mar-a-Lago on Tuesday night.

It’s the first test to see whether Trump can navigate two vasty different approaches to his legal case and his political campaign, which are now inextricably linked.

“He’s taking this very seriously and following legal advice,” an adviser to Trump said Monday night. “He will make his case to Americans Tuesday night at Mar-a-Lago.”

Alina Habba, who represents Donald Trump in several civil matters, spent time with the former president in New York today and said, “He’s in good spirits. Honestly, he’s as he normally would be. He’s ready to go in and do what he needs to do tomorrow.”

Habba — appearing on on Fox News’ “Jesse Watters” — was asked about the “game plan” for Trump’s appearance in court in Manhattan Tuesday and said, “It’s all mapped out.”

She added, “Barring any surprises, I think that it should be smooth. We’re trying to coordinate and cooperate with everybody to make sure that there are no problems.”

On whether Trump can get a fair trial in Manhattan, Habba said, “No, no. I think it’s very difficult. I’d like to have faith in this state, but I’ve been practicing for him now for a couple years and gone to court in New York for a few years, and I can tell you, it’s not the same as representing anybody else.”

Former President Donald Trump met with his attorneys Susan Necheles and Joe Tacopina at Trump Tower after arriving in New York City Monday, a source familiar with the meeting told CNN.

It is unclear if the newest addition to Trump’s New York legal team, Todd Blanche, attended the meeting.

Trump is expected to be arraigned Tuesday.

Former President Donald Trump has landed in New York’s La Guardia Airport ahead of his expected Tuesday arraignment in a Manhattan courthouse.

History was made Thursday when he was indicted by a New York grand jury. It’s the first time that a current or former US president has been criminally charged.

The Manhattan district attorney’s office has been investigating Trump’s alleged role in a hush money payment scheme and cover-up involving adult film star Stormy Daniels that dates to the 2016 presidential campaign.

Trump’s attorney said he plans to file “substantial” legal challenges. The former president has repeatedly denied wrongdoing in the matter and accused Democrats of targeting him politically.

A small group of supporters chanted as former President Donald Trump arrived at his namesake tower on Monday afternoon, with a handful of people waving banners with messages like “Trump won” and “Arrest Biden.”

While Trump couldn’t see his admirers as he entered a side door on 56th Street, his arrival came with more fanfare and security than at any other time in his post presidency.

Now that he is inside the building — where he launched his presidential bid nearly eight years ago — Trump is expected to meet with lawyers and political advisers on the eve of his arraignment, an aide told CNN.

Trump’s mood was described by an adviser as “defiant and focused,” as he watched expansive television coverage while flying back to his native New York City from Florida.

Special counsel prosecutors have secured evidence including daily notes, texts, emails and photographs and are focused on cataloging how Donald Trump handled classified records around the Mar-a-Lago resort and those who may have witnessed the former president with them, according to multiple sources familiar with the investigation.

The new details come amid signs the Justice Department is taking steps that are typically near the end of an investigation.

The recent investigative activity before a federal grand jury in Washington, DC, also includes subpoenaing witnesses in March and April who had previously spoken to investigators, the sources said. While the FBI interviewed many aides and workers at Mar-a-Lago nearly a year ago voluntarily, grand jury appearances are transcribed and under oath — an indication the prosecutors are locking in witness testimony.

The focus of both the mishandling of records and obstruction of justice probes has remained on the actions of the former president, the sources say. That includes prosecutors pursuing evidence of Trump’s intent to keep classified records after he left the White House, plus his knowledge that the records remained in his possession after the Justice Department demanded their return last May.

Witnesses are being questioned about what they saw in Trump’s private residential and work areas within the club, some of the sources said.

Former President Donald Trump has arrived at his midtown Manhattan property Trump Tower.

He will stay at Trump Tower tonight and is expected to depart New York immediately after Tuesday’s arraignment to head back to Florida — where he plans to deliver remarks at his Mar-a-lago home.

President Joe Biden told CNN’s Betsy Klein he isn’t worried about unrest due to former President Donald Trump’s criminal indictment in New York.

“No, I have faith in the New York Police Department,” Biden said on Monday during a tour of a power generation facility in Fridley, Minnesota.

Asked if he had faith in the legal system, Biden replied: “Yes.”

As former President Donald Trump walked down from his plane at La Guardia Airport on the eve of his arraignment, CNN’s Gloria Borger noted the unique situation he’s in.

Trump is “one of the most famous people in the world, and he is a former president, presidential candidate, and now he’s a defendant. We haven’t seen that. It’s hard to kind of process that,” Borger said to CNN’s Dana Bash.

Borger also highlighted how Trump’s day in court is something he’s avoided for decades and that it will be a grievance he uses to rile up his supporters.

“Donald Trump is somebody who’s probably tried to avoid his day in court for a very long time — decades. And now, you know, you have this man who is saying to people who support him, ‘You know I am your retribution.’ And he is going to make his grievance their grievance. And I think we’re going to see that play out over and over again, and we’ll see it play out in court with his attorney,” she said.

CNN’s Audie Cornish noted how Trump ending up in court seemed unavoidable given the “sheer number of investigations against him.”

Acting New York Supreme Court Judge Juan Merchan said he’ll likely issue a decision tonight on whether media outlets can broadcast former President Donald Trump’s arraignment on Tuesday.

CNN is part of the media coalition that asked the judge for permission to broadcast the hearing.

“As Your Honor is well-aware, this case presents extraordinary security concerns (including Secret Service-related concerns) and we submit that any video or photography of the proceedings will only heighten these serious concerns,” they wrote.

Donald Trump — the first former president in history to face criminal charges — has arrived in New York for an expected arraignment on Tuesday after being indicted last week by a Manhattan grand jury.

CNN’s John Miller, Jeremy Herb, Katelyn Polantz, Tierney Sneed, Sydney Kashiwagi, Kristen Holmes, Holmes Lybrand, Hannah Rabinowitz, Paula Reid, Alayna Treene, Gregory Clary and Devan Cole contributed reporting to this post.

Former President Donald Trump has landed in New York’s La Guardia Airport ahead of his expected Tuesday arraignment in a Manhattan courthouse.

The former president touched down shortly after 3:25 p.m. ET.

Trump faces more than 30 counts related to business fraud, CNN has reported, but the indictment remains under seal. He is the first current or former president to face criminal charges in American history.

He will spend Monday night at the Trump Tower, according to a source familiar with his plans.

The Secret Service is scheduled to accompany Trump in the early afternoon Tuesday to the district attorney’s office, which is in the same building as the courthouse, where he is expected to be arraigned.

The former president is expected to return to Florida shortly after his court appearance and has scheduled an event that evening to speak publicly.

Lawyers for former President Donald Trump told a New York judge on Monday that they oppose the request by media outlets to broadcast Trump’s arraignment on Tuesday.

“We submit that the media request should be denied because it will create a circus-like atmosphere at the arraignment, raise unique security concerns, and is inconsistent with President Trump’s presumption of innocence,” the Trump team said in a letter to New York Supreme Court Judge Juan Merchant.

Trump’s attorneys said in their letter to Acting New York Supreme Court Judge Juan Merchan that he is required under court rules to take into account potential security concerns.

“As Your Honor is well-aware, this case presents extraordinary security concerns (including Secret Service-related concerns) and we submit that any video or photography of the proceedings will only heighten these serious concerns,” they wrote.

Among the Trump lawyers signing the new letter was Todd Blanche, who, CNN reported, recently joined the former president’s legal team.

Meanwhile, the Manhattan District Attorney’s office also responded Monday to the news outlets’ request, and said that it was deferring to the judge to decide how best to manage his court room.

However, the district attorney’s office wrote that it would “be a defensible exercise of the Court’s discretion to exclude or restrict videography, photography, and radio coverage of the arraignment in the interest of avoiding potential prejudice to the defendant, maintaining an orderly proceeding, assuring the safety of the participants in the proceeding, or for other reasons within the Court’s broad authority to manage and control these proceedings.”

But the prosecutors also noted that there “does not appear to be a categorical prohibition on cameras during an arraignment” under existing New York statutes and case law.

While the district attorney’s office stopped short of opposing the request, its lawyer pointed out to Merchan that a similar request for audio-video broadcasting was made for the 2021 arraignment in the tax fraud case against Trump Organization and its CFO Allen Weisselberg.

The judge responded to that 2021 request by “allowing a limited number of still photographs to be taken prior to the commencement of proceedings,” the prosecutors noted Monday.

CNN is one of the outlets requesting camera access for Tuesday’s arraignment.

Former President Donald Trump has hired a new attorney to serve as lead counsel in his defense against charges brought by the Manhattan district attorney’s office, multiple sources tell CNN.

Trump has hired Todd Blanche, who was most recently a partner at law firm Cadwalader, Wickersham & Taft. Blanche has previously represented Trump campaign manager Paul Manafort and Igor Fruman, a former associate of Rudy Giuliani who was also a key figure in Trump’s first impeachment trial. Politico was first to report the news of Blanche’s hiring.

Trump attorney’s Joe Tacopina and Susan Necheles will remain on Trump’s legal team.

Blanche’s hiring has been seen by some inside Trump’s orbit as a sidelining of Tacopina, who up until this point has been one of the most forward-facing attorney’s dealing with Trump’s defense. However, a senior adviser to Trump pushed back on that characterization and said the addition of Blanche was intended to add more legal firepower to Trump’s defense.

When pressed by CNN’s Kaitlan Collins on Friday if Tacopina was the right lawyer in New York to take Trump to trial, another lawyer representing the former president, Tim Parlatore, pointed to a potential conflict of interest.

“I know that Joe has certain potential conflict issues, given his prior contacts, with Stormy Daniels. So, who’s the right attorney, to take it to trial, is something that the client will have to decide. Ultimately, the decision of who to stand next to, before a jury, is a decision that only the client can make,” Parlatore said.

Asked by CNN’s Paula Reid if he is still on the case and whether he will be in court tomorrow, Tacopina said: “Yes of course ! And I will be the lead trial lawyer when the bell rings!”

“As always, we will not allow violence or vandalism of any kind. And if one is caught participating in any act of violence, they will be arrested and held accountable. No matter who you are,” he added.
